3.2
Security Model

In this paper, we use the simulation-based paradigm of secure computation
[
26
]
to define and prove the proposed protocols. Since both types of (static) active and passive adversaries are involved in our protocols, we will provide formal definitions for both types. In this work, we consider a static adversary, we assume there is an authenticated private (off-chain) channel between the clients and we consider a standard public blockchain, e.g., Ethereum.

Two-party Computation.

A two-party protocol
\Gamma
problem is captured by specifying a random process that maps pairs of inputs to pairs of outputs, one for each party. Such process is referred to as a functionality denoted by
f:\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\times\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\times\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}
, where
f:=(f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
. For every input pair
(x,y)
, the output pair is a random variable
(f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,y),f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x,y))
, such that the party with input
x
wishes to obtain
f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,y)
while the party with input
y
wishes to receive
f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x,y)
. When
f
is deterministic, then
f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. In the setting where
f
is asymmetric and only one party (say the first one) receives the result,
f
is defined as
f:=(f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,y),\bot)
.

Security in the Presence of Passive Adversaries.

In the passive adversarial model, the party corrupted by such an adversary correctly follows the protocol specification. Nonetheless, the adversary obtains the internal state of the corrupted party, including the transcript of all the messages received, and tries to use this to learn information that should remain private. Loosely speaking, a protocol is secure if whatever can be computed by a party in the protocol can be computed using its input and output only. In the simulation-based model, it is required that a party’s view in a protocol’s
execution can be simulated given only its input and output. This implies that the parties learn nothing from the protocol’s execution. More formally, party
i
’s view (during the execution of
\Gamma
) on input pair
(x,y)
is denoted by
\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y)
and equals
(w,r^{\scriptscriptstyle i},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle i},...,m_{\scriptscriptstyle t}^{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
w\in\{x,y\}
is the input of
i^{\scriptscriptstyle th}
party,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is the outcome of this party’s internal random coin tosses, and
m_{\scriptscriptstyle j}^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
represents the
j^{\scriptscriptstyle th}
message this party receives. The output of the
i^{\scriptscriptstyle th}
party during the execution of
\Gamma
on
(x,y)
is denoted by
\mathsf{Output}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y)
and can be generated from its own view of the execution. The joint output of both parties is denoted by
\mathsf{Output}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y):=(\mathsf{Output}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y),\mathsf{Output}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y))
.

Definition 1

Let
f
be the deterministic functionality defined above. Protocol
\Gamma
security computes
f
in the presence of a static passive adversary if there exist polynomial-time algorithms
(\mathsf{Sim}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\mathsf{Sim}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
such that:

\{\mathsf{Sim}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,y))\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\equiv}}\{\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y)\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y}\\

\{\mathsf{Sim}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(y,f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x,y))\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\equiv}}\{\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y)\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y}

Security in the Presence of Active Adversaries.

In this adversarial model, the corrupted party may
arbitrarily deviate from the protocol specification, to learn the private inputs of the other parties or to influence the outcome of the computation. In this case, the adversary may not use the input provided. Therefore, beyond the possibility that a corrupted party may learn more than it should, correctness is also required. This means that a corrupted party must not be able to cause the output to be incorrectly distributed. Moreover, we require independence of inputs meaning that a corrupted party cannot make its input depend on the other party’s input. To capture the threats,
the security of a protocol is analyzed by comparing what an adversary can do in the real protocol to what it can do in an ideal scenario that is secure by definition. This is formalized by considering an ideal computation involving an incorruptible Trusted Third Party (TTP) to whom the parties send their inputs and receive the output of the ideal functionality. Below, we describe the executions in the ideal and real models.

First, we describe the execution in the ideal model. Let
P_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
P_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
be the parties participating in the
protocol,
i\in\{0,1\}
be the index of the corrupted party, and
\mathcal{A}
be a non-uniform
probabilistic polynomial-time adversary. Also, let
z
be an auxiliary input given to
\mathcal{A}
while
x
and
y
be the input of party
P_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
P_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
respectively. The honest party,
P_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, sends its received input to TTP. The corrupted party
P_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
may either abort (by replacing the input with a special abort message
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
), send its received input or send some other input of the same length to TTP. This decision is made by the adversary and may depend on the input value of
P_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
z
. If TTP receives
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, then it sends
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
to the honest party and the ideal execution terminates. Upon obtaining an input pair
(x,y)
, TTP computes
f_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x,y)
and
f_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x,y)
. It first sends
f_{\scriptscriptstyle i}(x,y)
to
P_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
which replies with “continue” or
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. In the former case, TTP sends
f_{\scriptscriptstyle j}(x,y)
to
P_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and in the latter it sends
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
to
P_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. The honest party always outputs the message that it obtained from TTP. A malicious party may output an arbitrary function of its initial inputs and the message it has obtained from TTP. The ideal execution of
f
on inputs
(x,y)
and
z
is denoted by
\mathsf{Ideal}^{\scriptscriptstyle f}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}(z),i}(x,y)
and is defined as the output pair of the honest party and
\mathcal{A}
from the above ideal execution. In the real model, the real two-party protocol
\Gamma
is executed
without the involvement of TTP. In this setting,
\mathcal{A}
sends all messages on
behalf of the corrupted party and may follow an arbitrary strategy.
The honest party follows the instructions of
\Gamma
. The real execution of
\Gamma
is denoted by
\mathsf{Real}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}(z),i}(x,y)
, it is defined as the joint output of the parties engaging in the real execution of
\Gamma
(on the inputs), in the presence of
\mathcal{A}
.

Next, we define security. At a high level, the definition states that a secure protocol in the real model emulates the ideal model. This is formulated by stating that adversaries in the ideal model can simulate executions of the protocol in the real model.

Definition 2

Let
f
be the two-party functionality defined above and
\Gamma
be a two-party protocol that computes
f
. Protocol
\Gamma
securely computes
f
with abort in the presence of static active adversaries if for every non-uniform probabilistic polynomial time adversary
\mathcal{A}
for the real model, there exists a non-uniform probabilistic polynomial-time adversary (or simulator)
\mathsf{Sim}
for the ideal model, such that for every
i\in\{0,1\}
, it holds that:

\{\mathsf{Ideal}^{\scriptscriptstyle f}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Sim}(z),i}(x,y)\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y,z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\equiv}}\{\mathsf{Real}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}(z),i}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(x,y)\}_{\scriptscriptstyle x,y,z}

3.3
Smart Contracts

Cryptocurrencies, such as Bitcoin
[
38
]
and Ethereum
[
46
]
, beyond offering a decentralised currency, support computations on transactions. In this setting, often a certain computation logic is encoded in a computer program, called a
“smart contract”
. To date, Ethereum is the most predominant cryptocurrency framework that enables users to define arbitrary smart contracts. In this framework, contract code is stored on the blockchain and executed by all parties (i.e., miners) maintaining the cryptocurrency, when the program inputs are provided by transactions. The program execution’s correctness is guaranteed by the security of the underlying blockchain components. To prevent a denial-of-service attack, the framework requires a transaction creator to pay a fee, called
“gas”
, depending on the complexity of the contract running on it.

3.4
Counter Collusion Smart Contracts

In order to let a party, e.g., a client, efficiently delegate a computation to a couple of potentially colluding third parties, e.g., servers, Dong
et al.

[
18
]
proposed two main smart contracts; namely, “Prisoner’s Contract” (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
) and “Traitor’s Contract” (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
).
The Prisoner’s contract is signed by the client and the servers. This contract tries to incentivize correct computation by using the following idea. It requires each server to pay a deposit before the computation is delegated. It is equipped with an external auditor that is invoked to detect a misbehaving server only when the servers provide non-equal results.

If a server behaves honestly, then it can withdraw its deposit. Nevertheless, if a cheating server is detected by the auditor, then (a portion) of its deposit is transferred to the client. If one of the servers is honest and the other one cheats, then the honest server receives a reward taken from the cheating server’s deposit. However, the dilemma, created by
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
between the two servers, can be addressed if they can make an enforceable promise, say via a “Colluder’s Contract” (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
), in which one party, called “ringleader”, would pay its counterparty a bribe if both follow the collusion and provide an incorrect computation to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. To counter
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
, Dong
et al.
proposed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
, which incentivises a colluding server to betray the other server and report the collusion without being penalised by
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. In this work, we slightly adjust and use these contracts. We have stated the related parameters of these tree contracts in Table
1
. We refer readers to Appendix
0.C
for the full description of the parameters and contracts.

3.5
Pseudorandom Function and Permutation

Informally, a pseudorandom function is a deterministic function that takes a key of length
\lambda
and an input; and outputs a value indistinguishable from that of a truly random function. In this paper, we use pseudorandom functions:
\mathtt{PRF}:\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda}\times\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, where
\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(p)=\lambda
is the security parameter. In practice, a pseudorandom function can be obtained from an efficient block cipher
[
30
]
.

The definition of a pseudorandom permutation,
\mathtt{PRP}:\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda}\times\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, is very similar to that of a pseudorandom function, with a difference; namely, it is required the keyed function
\mathtt{PRP}(k,.)
to be indistinguishable from a uniform permutation, instead of a uniform function.

3.6
Commitment Scheme

A commitment scheme involves a
sender
and a
receiver
. It also involves two phases; namely,
commit
and
open
. In the commit phase, the sender commits to a message:
x
as
\mathtt{Com}(x,r)=com
, that involves a secret value:
r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda}
. At the end of the commit phase, the commitment
{com}
is sent to the receiver. In the open phase, the sender sends the opening
\hat{x}:=(x,r)
to the receiver who verifies its correctness:
\mathtt{Ver}({com},\hat{x})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle?}}{{=}}1
and accepts if the output is
1
. A commitment scheme must satisfy two properties: (a)
hiding
: it is infeasible for an adversary (i.e., the receiver) to learn any information about the committed message
x
, until the commitment
{com}
is opened, and (b)
binding
: it is infeasible for an adversary (i.e., the sender) to open a commitment
{com}
to different values
\hat{x}^{\prime}:=(x^{\prime},r^{\prime})
than that was used in the commit phase, i.e., infeasible to find
\hat{x}^{\prime}
,
s.t.

\mathtt{Ver}({com},\hat{x})=\mathtt{Ver}({com},\hat{x}^{\prime})=1
, where
\hat{x}\neq\hat{x}^{\prime}
. There exist efficient commitment schemes both in (a) the standard model, e.g., Pedersen scheme
[
41
]
, and (b) the random oracle model using the well-known hash-based scheme such that committing is :
\mathtt{H}(x||r)={com}
and
\mathtt{Ver}({com},\hat{x})
requires checking:
\mathtt{H}(x||r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle?}}{{=}}{com}
, where
\mathtt{H}:\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle*}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda}
is a collision-resistant hash function, i.e., the probability to find
x
and
x^{\prime}
such that
\mathtt{H}(x)=\mathtt{H}(x^{\prime})
is negligible in the security parameter
\lambda
.

3.7
Hash Tables

A hash table is an array of bins each of which can hold a set of elements. It is accompanied by a hash function. To insert an element, we first compute the element’s hash, and then store the element in the bin whose index is the element’s hash. In this paper, we set the table’s parameters appropriately to ensure the number of elements in each bin does not exceed a predefined capacity. Given the maximum number of elements
c
and the bin’s maximum size
d
, we can determine the number of bins,
h
, by analysing hash tables under the balls into the bins model
[
8
]
. In Appendix
0.A
, we explain how the hash table parameters are set.

3.8
Merkle Tree

A Merkle tree is a data structure that supports a compact commitment of a set of values/blocks. As a result, it includes two parties, prover
\mathcal{P}
and verifier
\mathcal{V}
. The Merkle tree scheme includes three algorithms
(\mathtt{MT.genTree},\mathtt{MT.prove},

\mathtt{MT.verify})
, defined as follows:

\bullet

The algorithm that constructs a Merkle tree,
\mathtt{MT.genTree}
, is run by
\mathcal{V}
. It takes blocks,
u:=u_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,u_{\scriptscriptstyle n}
, as input. Then, it groups the blocks in pairs. Next, a collision-resistant hash function,
\mathtt{H}(.)
, is used to hash each pair. After that, the hash values are grouped in pairs and each pair is further hashed, and this process is repeated until only a single hash value, called “root”, remains. This yields a tree with the leaves corresponding to the input blocks and the root corresponding to the last remaining hash value.
\mathcal{V}
sends the root to
\mathcal{P}
.

\bullet

The proving algorithm,
\mathtt{MT.prove}
, is run by
\mathcal{P}
. It takes a block index,
i
, and a tree as inputs. It outputs a vector proof, of
\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(n)
elements. The proof asserts the membership of
i
-th block in the tree, and consists of all the sibling nodes on a path from the
i
-th block to the root of the Merkle tree (including
i
-th block). The proof is given to
\mathcal{V}
.

\bullet

The verification algorithm,
\mathtt{MT.verify}
, is run by
\mathcal{V}
. It takes as an input
i
-th block, a proof, and the tree’s root. It checks if the
i
-th block corresponds to the root. If the verification passes, it outputs
1
; otherwise, it outputs
0
.

The Merkle tree-based scheme has two properties:
correctness
and
security
. Informally, the correctness requires that if both parties run the algorithms correctly, then a proof is always accepted by
\mathcal{V}
. The security requires that a computationally bounded malicious
\mathcal{P}
cannot convince
\mathcal{V}
into accepting an incorrect proof, e.g., proof for a non-member block. The security relies on the assumption that it is computationally infeasible to find the hash function’s collision. Usually, for the sake of simplicity, it is assumed that the number of blocks,
n
, is a power of
2
. The height of the tree, constructed on
m
blocks, is
\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(n)
.

3.9
Polynomial Representation of Sets

The idea of using a polynomial to represent a set’s elements was proposed by Freedman
et al.
in
[
21
]
. Since then, the idea has been widely used, e.g., in
[
4
,
5
,
25
,
34
]
. In this representation, set elements
S=\{s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,s_{\scriptscriptstyle d}\}
are defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
and set
S
is represented as a polynomial of form:
\mathbf{p}(x)=\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
\mathbf{p}(x)\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
and
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
is a polynomial ring. Often a polynomial,
\mathbf{p}(x)
, of degree
d
is represented in the “coefficient form” as follows:
\mathbf{p}(x)=a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot x+...+a_{\scriptscriptstyle d}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle d}
. The form
\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{d}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
is a special case of the coefficient form. As shown in
[
10
,
34
]
, for two sets
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A)}
and
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(B)}
represented by polynomials
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
and
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
respectively, their product, which is polynomial
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
, represents the set union, while their greatest common divisor,
gcd(
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
,\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B})
, represents the set intersection. For two polynomials
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
and
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
of degree
d
, and two random polynomials
\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
of degree
d
, it is proven in
[
10
,
34
]
that:
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}+\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}\cdot\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B}=\bm{\mu}\cdot gcd(\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A},\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B})
, where
\bm{\mu}
is a uniformly random polynomial, and polynomial
\bm{\theta}
contains only information about the elements in
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A)}\cap S^{\scriptscriptstyle(B)}
, and contains no information about other elements in
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A)}
or
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(B)}
.

Given a polynomial
\bm{\theta}
that encodes sets intersection, one can find the set elements in the intersection via one of the following approaches. First, via polynomial evaluation: the party who already has one of the original input sets, say
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
, evaluates
\bm{\theta}
at every element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
of
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
and considers
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in the intersection if
\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
. Second, via polynomial root extraction: the party who does not have one of the original input sets, extracts the roots of
\bm{\theta}
, which contain the roots of (i) random polynomial
\bm{\mu}
and (ii) the polynomial that represents the intersection, i.e.,
gcd(\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle A},\mathbf{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle B})
. In this approach, to distinguish errors (i.e., roots of
\bm{\mu}
) from the intersection, PSIs in
[
1
,
34
]
use the
“hash-based padding technique”
. In this technique, every element
u_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in the set universe
\mathcal{U}
, becomes
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=u_{\scriptscriptstyle i}||\mathtt{H}(u_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
\mathtt{H}
is a cryptographic hash function with a sufficiently large output size. Given a field’s arbitrary element,
s\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
and
\mathtt{H}
’s output size
|\mathtt{H}(.)|
, we can parse
s
into
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, such that
s=x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}||x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
and
|x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|=|\mathtt{H}(.)|
. In a PSI that uses polynomial representation and this padding technique, after we extract each root of
\bm{\theta}
, say
s
, we parse it into
(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
and check
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}\mathtt{H}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})
. If the equation holds, then we consider
s
as an element of the intersection.

3.10
Horner’s Method

Horner’s method
[
19
]
allows for efficiently evaluating polynomials at a given point, e.g.,
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
. Specifically, given a polynomial of the form:
\tau(x)=a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot x+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+...+a_{\scriptscriptstyle d}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle d}
and a point:
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, one can efficiently evaluate the polynomial at
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
iteratively, in the following fashion:
\tau(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})=a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+...+x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(a_{\scriptscriptstyle d-1}+x_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\cdot a_{\scriptscriptstyle d})...)))
. Evaluating a polynomial of degree
d
naively requires
d
additions and
\frac{(d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+d)}{2}
multiplications. However, using Horner’s method the evaluation requires only
d
additions and
d
multiplications. We use this method throughout the paper.

3.11
Oblivious Linear Function Evaluation

Oblivious Linear function Evaluation (
\mathtt{OLE}
) is a two-party protocol that involves a sender and receiver. In
\mathtt{OLE}
, the sender has two inputs
a,b\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
and the receiver has a single input,
c\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
. The protocol allows the receiver to learn only
s=a\cdot c+b\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, while the sender learns nothing. Ghosh
et al.

[
23
]
proposed an efficient
\mathtt{OLE}
that has
O(1)
overhead and involves mainly symmetric key operations. Later, in
[
24
]
an enhanced
\mathtt{OLE}
, called
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
was proposed. The latter ensures that the receiver cannot learn anything about the sender’s inputs, even if it sets its input to
0
. In this paper, we use
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
. We refer readers to Appendix
0.B
, for its construction.

3.12
Coin-Tossing Protocol

A Coin-Tossing protocol,
\mathtt{CT}
, allows two mutually distrustful parties, say
A
and
B
, to jointly generate a single random bit. Formally,
\mathtt{CT}
computes the functionality
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}(in_{\scriptscriptstyle A},in_{\scriptscriptstyle B})\rightarrow(out_{\scriptscriptstyle A},out_{\scriptscriptstyle B})
, which takes
in_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
and
in_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
as inputs of
A
and
B
respectively and outputs
out_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
to
A
and
out_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
to
B
, where
out_{\scriptscriptstyle A}=out_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
. A basic security requirement of a
\mathtt{CT}
is that the resulting bit is (computationally) indistinguishable from a truly random bit.

Blum proposed a simple
\mathtt{CT}
in
[
9
]
that works as follows. Party
A
picks a random bit
in_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\{0,1\}
, commits to it and sends the commitment to
B
which sends its choice of random input,
in_{\scriptscriptstyle B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\{0,1\}
, to
A
. Then,
A
sends the opening of the commitment (including
in_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
) to
B
, which checks whether the commitment matches its opening. If so, each party computes the final random bit as
in_{\scriptscriptstyle A}\oplus in_{\scriptscriptstyle B}
.

There have also been
fair
coin-tossing protocols, e.g., in
[
37
]
, that ensure either both parties learn the result or nobody does. These protocols can be generalised to
multi-party
coin-tossing protocols to generate a
random string
(rather than a single bit), e.g., see
[
6
,
32
]
.
The overall computation and communication complexities of (fair) multi-party coin-tossing protocols are often linear with the number of participants. In this paper, any secure multi-party
\mathtt{CT}
that generates a random string can be used. For the sake of simplicity, we let a multi-party
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
take
m
inputs and output a single value, i.e.,
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}(in_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,in_{\scriptscriptstyle m})\rightarrow out
.

4
Definition of Multi-party PSI with Fair Compensation

In this section, we present the notion of multi-party PSI with Fair Compensation (
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
) which allows either all clients to get the result or the honest parties to be financially compensated if the protocol aborts in an unfair manner, where only dishonest parties learn the result.

In a
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
, three types of parties are involved; namely, (1) a set of clients
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
potentially
malicious
(i.e., active adversaries) and all but one may collude with each other, (2) a non-colluding dealer,
D
, potentially semi-honest (i.e., a passive adversary) and has an input set, and (3) an auditor
{Aud}
potentially semi-honest, where all parties except
{Aud}
have input set. For simplicity, we assume that given an address one can determine whether it belongs to
{Aud}
.

The basic functionality that any multi-party PSI computes can be defined as
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}(S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1})\rightarrow\underbrace{(S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap})}_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1}
, where
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}=S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cap S_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},...,\cap\ S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1}
. To formally define a
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
, we equip the above PSI functionality with four predicates,
Q:=(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
, which ensure that certain financial conditions are met.
We borrow three of these predicates (i.e.,
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
) from the “fair and robust multi-party computation” initially proposed in
[
33
]
; nevertheless, we will (i) introduce an additional predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}
and (ii) provide more formal accurate definitions of these predicates.

Predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}
specifies under which condition a protocol that realises
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
should start executing, i.e., when all set owners have enough deposit. Predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}
determines in which situation parties receive their output, i.e., when honest parties receive their deposit back. Predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
specifies under which condition the simulator can force parties to abort if the adversary learns the output, i.e., when an honest party receives its deposit back plus a predefined amount of compensation. Predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}
specifies under which condition the simulator can force parties to abort if the adversary receives no output, i.e., when honest parties receive their deposits back. We observed that the latter predicate should have been defined in the generic framework in
[
33
]
too; as the framework should have also captured the cases where an adversary may abort without learning any output after the onset of the protocol. Intuitively, by requiring any protocol that realises
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
to implement a wrapped version of
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
that includes
Q
, we will ensure that an honest set owner only aborts in an unfair manner if
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
returns
1
, it only aborts in a fair manner if
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}
returns
1
, and outputs a valid value if
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}
returns
1
. Now, we formally define each of these predicates.

Definition 3
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}
: Initiation predicate)

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a stable ledger,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
be smart contract
sc
’s address,
Adr
be a set of
m+1
distinct addresses, and
\ddot{x}
be a fixed amount of coins. Then, predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},m+1,Adr,\ddot{x})
returns
1
if every address in
Adr
has at least
\ddot{x}
coins in
sc
; otherwise, it returns
0
.

Definition 4
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}
:
Delivery predicate)

Let
pram:=(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},\ddot{x})
be the parameters defined above, and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Adr
be the address of an honest party.
Then, predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}(pram,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
returns
1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
has sent
\ddot{x}
amount to
sc
and received
\ddot{x}
amount from it; thus, its balance in
sc
is
0
. Otherwise, it returns
0
.

Definition 5
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
: UnFair-Abort predicate)

Let
pram:=(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},\ddot{x})
be the parameters defined above, and
Adr^{\prime}\subset Adr
be a set containing honest parties’ addresses,
m^{\prime}=|Adr^{\prime}|
, and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Adr^{\prime}
. Let also
G
be a compensation function that takes as input three parameters
(\ddot{deps},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},m^{\prime})
, where
\ddot{deps}
is the amount of coins that all
m+1
parties deposit. It returns the amount of compensation each honest party must receive, i.e.,
G(\ddot{deps},ard_{\scriptscriptstyle i},m^{\prime})\rightarrow\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. Then, predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
is defined as
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}(pram,G,\ddot{deps},m^{\prime},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\rightarrow(a,b)
, where
a=1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is an honest party’s address and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
has sent
\ddot{x}
amount to
sc
and received
\ddot{x}+\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
from it, and
b=1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is
{Aud}
’s address and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
received
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
from
sc
. Otherwise,
a=b=0
.

Definition 6
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}
: Fair-Abort predicate)

Let
pram:=(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},\ddot{x})
be the parameters defined above, and
Adr^{\prime}\subset Adr
be a set containing honest parties’ addresses,
m^{\prime}=|Adr^{\prime}|
,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Adr^{\prime}
, and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
be
{Aud}
’s address. Let
G
be the compensation function, defined above and let
G(deps,ard_{\scriptscriptstyle j},m^{\prime})\rightarrow\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
be the compensation that the auditor must receive. Then, predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}(pram,G,\ddot{deps},m^{\prime},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
returns
1
, if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
(s.t.
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\neq adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
) has sent
\ddot{x}
amount to
sc
and received
\ddot{x}
from it, and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
received
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
from
sc
. Otherwise, it returns
0
.

Next, we present a formal definition of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
.

Definition 7
(
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
)

Let
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
be the multi-party PSI functionality defined above. We say protocol
\Gamma
realises
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with
Q
-fairness in the presence of
m-1
static active-adversary clients (i.e.,
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s) or a static passive dealer
D
or passive auditor
Aud
, if for every non-uniform probabilistic polynomial time adversary
\mathcal{A}
for the real model, there exists a non-uniform probabilistic polynomial-time adversary (or simulator)
\mathsf{Sim}
for the ideal model, such that for every
I\in\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m},D,Aud\}
, it holds that:

\{\mathsf{Ideal}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{W}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}},Q)}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Sim}(z),I}(S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1})\}_{\scriptscriptstyle S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1},z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\equiv}}\{\mathsf{Real}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}(z),I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1})\}_{\scriptscriptstyle S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1},z}

where
z
is an auxiliary input given to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{W}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}},Q)
is a functionality that wraps
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with predicates
Q:=(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
.

5
Other Subroutines Used in Justitia

In this section, we present three subroutines and a primitive that we developed and are used in the instantiation of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
, i.e., Justitia.

5.1
Verifiable Oblivious Polynomial Randomisation (
\mathtt{VOPR}
)

In the
\mathtt{VOPR}
, two parties are involved, (i) a sender which is potentially a passive adversary and (ii) a receiver that is potentially an active adversary. The protocol allows the receiver with input polynomial
\bm{\beta}
(of degree
e^{\prime}
) and the sender with input random polynomials
\bm{\psi}
(of degree
e
) and
\bm{\alpha}
(of degree
e+e^{\prime}
) to compute:
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}
, such that (a) the receiver learns only
\bm{\theta}
and nothing about the sender’s input even if it sets
\bm{\beta}=0
, (b) the sender learns nothing, and (c) the receiver’s misbehaviour is detected in the protocol. Thus, the functionality that
\mathtt{VOPR}
computes is defined as
f^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathtt{VOPR}}}((\bm{\psi},\bm{\alpha}),\bm{\beta})\rightarrow(\bot,\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha})
.
We will use
\mathtt{VOPR}
in Justitia for two main reasons: (a) to let a party re-randomise its counterparty’s polynomial (representing its set) and (b) to impose a MAC-like structure to the randomised polynomial; such a structure will allow a verifier to detect if
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s output has been modified.

Now, we outline how we design
\mathtt{VOPR}
without using any (expensive) zero-knowledge proofs.
4
4

4

Previously, Ghosh
et al.

[
24
]
designed a protocol called Oblivious Polynomial Addition (OPA) to meet similar security requirements that we laid out above. But, as shown in
[
3
]
, OPA is susceptible to several serious attacks.
In the setup phase, both parties represent their input polynomials in the regular coefficient form; therefore, the sender’s polynomials are defined as
\bm{\psi}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
\bm{\alpha}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e+e^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and the receiver’s polynomial is defined as
\bm{\beta}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}b_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, where
b_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\neq 0
. However, the sender computes each coefficient
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
(of polynomial
\bm{\alpha}
) as follows,
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\sum\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle k=e^{\prime}\\
\scriptscriptstyle t=e\end{subarray}}_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k=0}a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}
, where
t+k=j
and each
a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}
is a random value. For instance, if
e=4
and
e^{\prime}=3
, then
a_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}=a_{\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle 0,3}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 3,0}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,2}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,1}
. Shortly, we explain why polynomial
\bm{\alpha}
is constructed this way.

In the computation phase, to compute polynomial
\bm{\theta}
, the two parties interactively multiply and add the related coefficients in a secure way using
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
(presented in Section
3.11
). Specifically,
for every
j
(where
0\leq j\leq e^{\prime}
) the sender sends
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
to an instance of
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
, while the receiver sends
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
to the same instance, which returns
c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
to the receiver. This process is repeated for every
i
, where
0\leq i\leq e
. Then, the receiver uses
c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
values to construct the resulting polynomial,
\bm{\theta}
.

The reason that the sender imposes the above structure to (the coefficients of)
\bm{\alpha}
in the setup, is to let the parties securely compute
\bm{\theta}
via
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
. Specifically, by imposing this structure (1) the sender can blind each product
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
with random value
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
which is a component of
\bm{\alpha}
’s coefficient and (2) the receiver can construct a result polynomial of the form
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}
.

Now, we outline how the verification works. To check the result’s correctness, the sender picks and sends a random value
z
to the receiver which computes
\bm{\theta}(z)
and
\bm{\beta}(z)
and sends these two values to the sender. The sender computes
\bm{\psi}(z)
and
\bm{\alpha}(z)
and then checks if equation
\bm{\theta}({z})=\bm{\psi}({z})\cdot\bm{\beta}({z})+\bm{\alpha}({z})
holds. It accepts the result if the check passes.

Figure
1
describes
\mathtt{VOPR}
in detail. Note,
\mathtt{VOPR}
requires that the sender to insert non-zero coefficients, i.e.,
b_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\neq 0
for all
i,0\leq i\leq e^{\prime}
. If a sender inserts a zero-coefficient, then it will learn only a random value (due to
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
), accordingly it cannot pass
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s verification phase. However, such a requirement will not affect Justitia’s correctness, as we will discuss in Section
6.2
and Appendix
0.E
.

\bullet

Input.

\bullet

Public Parameters
: upper bound on input polynomials’ degree:
e
and
e^{\prime}
.

\bullet

Sender Input
: random polynomials:
\bm{\psi}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
\bm{\alpha}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e+e^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Each
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
has the form:
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\sum\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle k=e^{\prime}\\
\scriptscriptstyle t=e\end{subarray}}_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k=0}a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}
, such that
t+k=j
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

\bullet

Receiver Input
: polynomial
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}b_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, where
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is a random polynomial of degree
1
,
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is an arbitrary polynomial of degree
e^{\prime}-1
, and
b_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\neq 0
.

\bullet

Output.
The receiver gets
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}
.

1.

Computation:

(a)

Sender and receiver together for every
j
,
0\leq j\leq e^{\prime}
, invoke
e+1
instances of
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
. In particular,
\forall j,0\leq j\leq e^{\prime}
: sender sends
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
while the receiver sends
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
to
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
that returns:
c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
to the receiver (
\forall i,0\leq i\leq e
).

(b)

The receiver sums component-wise values
c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
that results in polynomial:

\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}=\sum\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle i=e\\
\scriptscriptstyle j=e^{\prime}\end{subarray}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j=0}c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i+j}

2.

Verification:

(a)

Sender: picks a random (non-zero) value
z
and sends it to the receiver.

(b)

Receiver: sends
\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\theta}(z)
and
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\beta}(z)
to the sender.

(c)

Sender: computes
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\psi}(z)
and
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\alpha}(z)
and checks if equation
\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\psi{\scriptscriptstyle z}\cdot\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
holds. If the equation holds, it concludes that the computation was performed correctly. Otherwise, it aborts.

Figure 1:
Verifiable Oblivious Polynomial Randomization (
\mathtt{VOPR}
)

Theorem 5.1

Let
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}
be the functionality defined above. If the enhanced
\mathtt{OLE}
(i.e.,
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
) is secure against malicious (or active) adversaries, then the Verifiable Oblivious Polynomial Randomisation (
\mathtt{VOPR}
), presented in Figure
1
, securely computes
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}
in the presence of (i) a semi-honest sender and honest receiver or (ii) a malicious receiver and honest sender.

Proof

Before proving Theorem
5.1
, we present Lemma
1
and Theorem
5.2
that will be used in the proof of Theorem
5.1
.
Informally, Lemma
1
states that the evaluation of a random polynomial at a fixed value results in a uniformly random value.

Lemma 1

Let
x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
be an element of a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, picked uniformly at random and
\bm{\mu}(x)
be a random polynomial of constant degree
d
(where
d=const(p)
) and defined over
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
.
Then, the evaluation of
\bm{\mu}(x)
at
x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is distributed uniformly at random over the elements of the field, i.e.,
Pr[\bm{\mu}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=y]=\frac{1}{p}
, where
y\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
.

Proof

Let
\bm{\mu}(x)=a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}+\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle d}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where the coefficients are distributed uniformly at random over the field.

Then, for any choice of
x
and random coefficients
a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,a_{\scriptscriptstyle d}
, it holds that:

Pr[\bm{\mu}(x)=y]=Pr[\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle d}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}=y]=Pr[a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=y-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle d}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}]=\frac{1}{p}

\forall y\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, as
a_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
has been picked uniformly at random from
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
.

\Box

Informally, Theorem
5.2
states that the product of two arbitrary polynomials (in coefficient form) is a polynomial whose roots are the union of the two original polynomials. Below, we formally state it. The theorem has been taken from
[
3
]
.

Theorem 5.2

Let
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
be two arbitrary non-constant polynomials of degree
d
and
d^{\prime}
respectively, such that
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
and they are in coefficient form. Then, the product of the two polynomials is a polynomial whose roots include precisely the two polynomials’ roots.

We refer readers to Appendix
0.D
for the proof of Theorem
5.2
. Next, we prove the main theorem, i.e., Theorem
5.1
, by considering the case where each party is corrupt, in turn.

Case 1: Corrupt sender.
In the real execution, the sender’s view is defined as follows:

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}\Big((\bm{\psi},\bm{\alpha}),\bm{\beta}\Big)=\{\bm{\psi},\bm{\alpha},r_{\scriptscriptstyle S},\bm{\beta}(z),\bm{\theta}(z),\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S},\bot\}

where
r_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
is the outcome of internal random coins of the sender and
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
refers to the sender’s real-model view during the execution of
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
. The simulator
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
, which receives
\bm{\psi}
and
\bm{\alpha}
, works as follows.

1.

generates an empty view. It appends to the view polynomials
(\bm{\psi}
,
\bm{\alpha})
and coins
r^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
chosen uniformly at random.

2.

computes polynomial
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, where
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is a random polynomial of degree
1
and
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is an arbitrary polynomial of degree
e^{\prime}-1
. Next, it constructs polynomial
\bm{\theta}
as follows:
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}
.

3.

picks value
z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Then, it evaluates polynomials
\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\theta}
at point
z
. This results in values
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
respectively. It appends these two values to the view.

4.

extracts the sender-side simulation of
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
from
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
’s simulator. Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
be this simulation. Note, the latter simulation is guaranteed to exist, as
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
has been proven secure (in
[
24
]
). It appends
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
and
\bot
to its view.

Now, we are ready to show that the two views are computationally indistinguishable. The sender’s inputs are identical in both models, so they have identical distributions. Since the real-model semi-honest adversary samples its randomness according to the protocol’s description, the random coins in both models have identical distributions. Next, we explain why values
\bm{\beta}(z)
in the real model and
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
in the ideal model are (computationally) indistinguishable. In the real model,
\bm{\beta}(z)
is the evaluation of polynomial
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
at random point
z
, where
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is a random polynomial. We know that
\bm{\beta}(z)=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(z)\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(z)
, for any (non-zero)
z
. Moreover, by Lemma
1
, we know that
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(z)
is a uniformly random value. Therefore,
\bm{\beta}(z)=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(z)\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(z)
is a uniformly random value as well. In the ideal world, polynomial
\bm{\beta}
has the same structure as
\bm{\beta}
has (i.e.,
\bm{\beta}=\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, where
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is a random polynomial). That means
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
is a uniformly random value too. Thus,
\bm{\beta}(z)
and
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
are computationally indistinguishable. Next, we turn our attention to values
\bm{\theta}(z)
in the real model and
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
in the ideal model. We know that
\bm{\theta}(z)
is a function of
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(z)
, as polynomial
\bm{\theta}
has been defined as
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{(}\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})+\bm{\alpha}
. Similarly,
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
is a function of
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
. As we have already discussed,
\bm{\beta}(z)
and
\bm{\beta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
are computationally indistinguishable, so are their functions
\bm{\theta}(z)
and
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
. Moreover, as
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
has been proven secure,
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
are computationally indistinguishable. It is also clear that
\bot
is identical in both models. We conclude that the two views are computationally indistinguishable.

Case 2: Corrupt receiver.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
be the simulator, in this case, which uses a subroutine adversary,
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
works as follows.

1.

simulates
{\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
and receives
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
’s input coefficients
b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
for all
j
,
0\leq j\leq e^{\prime}
, as we are in
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-hybrid model.

2.

reconstructs polynomial
\bm{\beta}
, given the above coefficients.

3.

simulates the honest sender’s inputs as follows.
It picks two random polynomials:
{\bm{\psi}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}{g}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
{\bm{\alpha}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle e+e^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, such that
{g}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
and every
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
has the form:
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\sum\limits^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle k=e^{\prime}\\
\scriptscriptstyle t=e\end{subarray}}_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k=0}a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}
, where
t+k=j
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle t,k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
.

4.

sends to
{\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
’s functionality values
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
and receives
c_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
from this functionality (for all
i,j
).

5.

sends all
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
to TTP and receives polynomial
{\bm{\theta}}
.

6.

picks a random value
z
from
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
. Then, it computes
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}={\bm{\psi}}(z)
and
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}={\bm{\alpha}}(z)
.

7.

sends
z
and all
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
which sends back
\theta_{z}
and
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
to the simulator.

8.

sends
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
.

9.

checks if the following relation hold:

\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{z}}={\bm{\beta}}(z)\hskip 17.07164pt\wedge\hskip 17.07164pt\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}={\bm{\theta}}(z)\hskip 17.07164pt\wedge\hskip 17.07164pt{\bm{\theta}}(z)=\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\cdot\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}

(1)

If Relation
1
does not hold, it aborts (i.e., sends abort signal
\Lambda
to the sender) and still proceeds to the next step.

10.

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
outputs.

We first focus on the adversary’s output. Both values of
z
in the real and ideal models have been picked uniformly at random from
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
; therefore, they have identical distributions. In the real model, values
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
are the result of the evaluations of two random polynomials at (random) point
z
. In the ideal model, values
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
are also the result of the evaluations of two random polynomials (i.e.,
{\bm{\psi}}
and
{\bm{\alpha}}
) at point
z
. By Lemma
1
, we know that the evaluation of a random polynomial at an arbitrary value
yields a uniformly random value in
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
. Therefore, the distribution of pair
(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z},\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z})
in the real model is identical to that of pair
(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z},\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z})
in the ideal model. Moreover, the final result (i.e., values
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
) in the real model has the same distribution as the final result (i.e., values
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
) in the ideal model, as they are the outputs of the ideal calls to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
, as we are in the
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}}
-hybrid model.

Next, we turn our attention to the sender’s output. We will show that the output distributions of the honest sender in the ideal and real models are statistically close.
Our focus will be on the probability that it aborts in each model, as it does not receive any other output. In the ideal model,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
is given the honestly generated result polynomial
{\bm{\theta}}
(computed by TTP) and the adversary’s input polynomial
{\bm{\beta}}
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
aborts with a probability of 1 if Relation
1
does not hold. However, in the real model, the honest sender (in addition to its inputs) is given only
\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and
\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
and is not given polynomials
{\bm{\beta}}
and
{\bm{\theta}}
; it wants to check if the following equation holds,
\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\cdot\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
. Note, polynomial
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}+\bm{\alpha}
(resulted from
{c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
) is well-structured, as it satisfies the following three conditions, regardless of the adversary’s input
\bm{\beta}
to
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
, (i)
deg(\bm{\theta})=Max\Big(deg(\bm{\beta})+deg(\bm{\psi}),deg(\bm{\alpha})\Big)
, as
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
is an integral domain and (
\bm{\psi},\bm{\alpha}
) are random polynomials, (ii) the roots of the product polynomial
\bm{\nu}=\bm{\psi}\cdot\bm{\beta}
contains exactly both polynomials’ roots, by Theorem
5.2
, and (iii) the roots of
\bm{\nu}+\bm{\alpha}
is the intersection of the roots of
\bm{\nu}
and
\bm{\alpha}
, as shown in
[
34
]
. Furthermore, polynomial
\bm{\theta}
reveals no information (about
\bm{\psi}
and
\bm{\alpha}
except their degrees) to the adversary and the pair
(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle z},\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z})
is given to the adversary after it sends the pair
(\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z},\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z})
to the sender.
There are exactly four cases where pair
(\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z},\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z})
can be constructed by the real-model adversary. Below, we state each case and the probability that the adversary is detected in that case during the verification, i.e.,
\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle?}}{{=}}\psi{\scriptscriptstyle z}\cdot\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
.

1.

\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\theta}(z)\wedge\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}={\bm{\beta}}(z)
. This is a trivial non-interesting case, as the adversary has behaved honestly, so it can always pass the verification.

2.

\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq\bm{\theta}(z)\wedge\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}={\bm{\beta}}(z)
. In this case, the adversary is detected with a probability of 1.

3.

\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}=\bm{\theta}(z)\wedge\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq{\bm{\beta}}(z)
. In this case, the adversary is also detected with a probability of 1.

4.

\theta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq\bm{\theta}(z)\wedge\beta_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq{\bm{\beta}}(z)
. In this case, the adversary is detected with an overwhelming probability, i.e.,
1-\frac{1}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 2\lambda}}
.

As we illustrated above, in the real model, the lowest probability that the honest sender would abort in case of adversarial behaviour is
1-\frac{1}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 2\lambda}}
. Thus, the honest sender’s output distributions in the ideal and real models are statistically close, i.e.,
1

vs

1-\frac{1}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 2\lambda}}
.

We conclude that the distribution of the joint outputs of the honest sender and adversary in the real and ideal models are computationally indistinguishable.

\Box

5.2
Zero-sum Pseudorandom Values Agreement Protocol (
\mathtt{ZSPA}
)

The
\mathtt{ZSPA}
allows
m
parties (the majority of which is potentially malicious) to efficiently agree on (a set of vectors, where each
i
-th vector has)
m
pseudorandom values such that their sum equals zero. At a high level, the parties first sign a smart contract, register their accounts/addresses in it, and then run a coin-tossing protocol
\mathtt{CT}
to agree on a key:
k
. Next, one of the parties generates
m-1
pseudorandom values
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
(where
1\leq j\leq m-1
) using key
k
and
\mathtt{PRF}
. It sets the last value as the additive inverse of the sum of the values generated, i.e.
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,m}=-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
(similar to the standard XOR-based secret sharing
[
43
]
).
Then, it constructs a Merkel tree on top of the pseudorandom values and stores only the tree’s root
g
and the key’s hash value
q
in the smart contract. Then, each party (using the key) locally checks if the values (on the contract) have been constructed correctly; if so, then it sends a (signed) “approved” message to the contract which only accepts messages from registered parties. Hence, the functionality that
\mathtt{ZSPA}
computes is defined as
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}\underbrace{(\bot,...,\bot)}_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\rightarrow\underbrace{((k,g,q),...,(k,g,q))}_{\scriptscriptstyle m}
, where
g
is the Markle tree’s root built on the pseudorandom values
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
,
q
is the hash value of the key used to generate the pseudorandom values, and
m\geq 2
. Figure
2
presents
\mathtt{ZSPA}
in detail.

Briefly,
\mathtt{ZSPA}
will be used in Justitia to allow clients
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
to provably agree on a set of pseudorandom values, where each set represents a pseudorandom polynomial (as the elements of the set are considered the polynomial’s coefficients). Due to
\mathtt{ZSPA}
’s property, the sum of these polynomials is zero. Each of these polynomials will be used by a client to blind/encrypt the messages it sends to the smart contract, to protect the privacy of the plaintext message (from
{Aud}
, D, and the public). To compute the sum of the plaintext messages, one can easily sum the blinded messages, which removes the blinding polynomials.

\bullet

Parties. A set of clients
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
.

\bullet

Input.
m
: the total number of participants,
adr
: an address of a deployed smart contract which maintains the clients’ addresses and accepts messages only from them, and
b
: the total number of vectors. Let
b^{\prime}=b-1
.

\bullet

Output.
k
: a secret key that generates
b
vectors
[z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,m}],...,[z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},m}]
of pseudorandom values,
q
: hash of the key,
g
: a Merkle tree’s root, and a vector of (signed) messages.

1.

Coin-tossing.

\mathtt{CT}(in_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,in_{\scriptscriptstyle m})\rightarrow k
.

All participants run a coin-tossing protocol to agree on
\mathtt{PRF}
’s key,
k
.

2.

Encoding.

\mathtt{Encode}(k,m)\rightarrow(g,q)
.

One of the parties takes the following steps:

(a)

for every
i
(where
0\leq i\leq b^{\prime}
), generates
m
pseudorandom values as follows.

\forall j,1\leq j\leq m-1:z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{PRF}(k,i||j),\hskip 14.22636ptz_{\scriptscriptstyle i,m}=-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}

(b)

constructs a Merkel tree on top of all pseudorandom values,
\mathtt{MT.genTree}(z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},m})\rightarrow g
.

(c)

sends the Merkel tree’s root:
g
, and the key’s hash:
q=\mathtt{H}(k)
to
adr
.

3.

Verification.

\mathtt{Verify}(k,g,q,m,flag)\rightarrow(a,s)
.
Each party
A_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
checks if, all
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
values, the root
g
, and key’s hash
q
have been correctly generated, by retaking step
2
.

•

If the checks pass, then it sets
a=1
and

–

if
flag=A
, where
A\in\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
, then it sets
s
to a (signed) “approved” message, and sends
s
to
adr

–

if
flag=A
, where
A\notin\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
, then it sets
s=\bot
.

•

If the checks do not pass, it aborts by returning
a=0
and
s=\bot
.

Figure 2:
Zero-sum Pseudorandom Values Agreement (
\mathtt{ZSPA}
). The use of
flag
allows an external party (e.g., an auditor) to locally run the verification without having to send any messages to the smart contract.

Theorem 5.3

Let
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
be the functionality defined above. If
\mathtt{CT}
is secure against a malicious adversary and the correctness of
\mathtt{PRF}
,
\mathtt{H}
, and Merkle tree holds, then
\mathtt{ZSPA}
, in Figure
2
, securely computes
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
in the presence of
m-1
malicious adversaries.

Proof

For the sake of simplicity, we will assume the sender, which generates the result, sends the result directly to the rest of the parties, i.e., receivers, instead of sending it to a smart contract. We first consider the case in which the sender is corrupt.

Case 1: Corrupt sender.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
be the simulator using a subroutine adversary,
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
works as follows.

1.

simulates
\mathtt{CT}
and receives the output value
k
from
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
, as we are in
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
-hybrid model.

2.

sends
k
to TTP and receives back from it
m
pairs, where each pair is of the form
(g,q)
.

3.

sends
k
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
and receives back from it
m
pairs where each pair is of the form
(g^{\prime},q^{\prime})
.

4.

checks whether the following equations hold (for each pair):
g=g^{\prime}\hskip 5.69054pt\wedge\hskip 5.69054ptq=q^{\prime}
. If the two equations do not hold, then it aborts (i.e., sends abort signal
\Lambda
to the receiver) and proceeds to the next step.

5.

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
outputs.

We first focus on the adversary’s output. In the real model, the only messages that the adversary receives are those messages it receives as the result of the ideal call to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
. These messages have identical distribution to the distribution of the messages in the ideal model, as the
\mathtt{CT}
is secure. Now, we move on to the receiver’s output. We will show that the output distributions of the honest receiver in the ideal and real models are computationally indistinguishable. In the real model, each element of pair
(g,p)
is the output of a deterministic function on the output of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
. We know the output of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
in the real and ideal models have an identical distribution, and so do the evaluations of deterministic functions (i.e., Merkle tree,
\mathtt{H}
, and
\mathtt{PRF}
) on them, as long as these three functions’ correctness holds. Therefore, each pair
(g,q)
in the real model has an identical distribution to pair
(g,q)
in the ideal model. For the same reasons, the honest receiver in the real model aborts with the same probability as
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
does in the ideal model. We conclude that the distributions of the joint outputs of the adversary and honest receiver in the real and ideal models are (computationally) indistinguishable.

Case 2: Corrupt receiver.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
be the simulator that uses subroutine adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
works as follows.

1.

simulates
\mathtt{CT}
and receives the output value
k
from
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
.

2.

sends
k
to TTP and receives back
m
pairs of the form
(g,q)
from TTP.

3.

sends
(k,g,q)
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
and outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
outputs.

In the real model, the adversary receives two sets of messages, the first set includes the transcripts (including
k
) it receives when it makes an ideal call to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
and the second set includes pair
(g,q)
. As we already discussed above (because we are in the
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
-hybrid model) the distributions of the messages it receives from
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
in the real and ideal models are identical. Moreover, the distribution of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
’s output (i.e.,
\bar{k}
and
k
) in both models is identical; therefore, the honest sender’s output distribution in both models is identical. As we already discussed, the evaluations of deterministic functions (i.e., Merkle tree,
\mathtt{H}
, and
\mathtt{PRF}
) on
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
’s outputs have an identical distribution. Therefore, each pair
(g,q)
in the real model has an identical distribution to the pair
(g,q)
in the ideal model. Hence, the distribution of the joint outputs of the adversary and honest receiver in the real and ideal models is indistinguishable.

\Box

In addition to the security guarantee (i.e., computation’s correctness against malicious sender or receiver) stated by Theorem
5.3
,
\mathtt{ZSPA}
offers (a) privacy against the public, and (b) non-refutability. Informally, privacy here means that given the state of the contract (i.e.,
g
and
q
), an external party cannot learn any information about any of the pseudorandom values,
z_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
; while non-refutability means that if a party sends “approved” then in future cannot deny the knowledge of the values whose representation is stored in the contract.

Theorem 5.4

If
\mathtt{H}
is preimage resistance,
\mathtt{PRF}
is secure, the signature scheme used in the smart contract is secure (i.e., existentially unforgeable under chosen message attacks), and the blockchain is secure (i.e., offers persistence and liveness properties
[
22
]
) then
\mathtt{ZSPA}
offers (i) privacy against the public and (ii) non-refutability.

Proof

First, we focus on privacy. Since key
k
, for
\mathtt{PRF}
, has been picked uniformly at random and
\mathtt{H}
is preimage resistance, the probability that given
g
the adversary can find
k
is negligible in the security parameter, i.e.,
\epsilon(\lambda)
. Furthermore, because
\mathtt{PRF}
is secure (i.e., its outputs are indistinguishable from random values) and
\mathtt{H}
is preimage resistance, given the Merkle tree’s root
g
, the probability that the adversary can find a leaf node, which is the output of
\mathtt{PRF}
, is
\epsilon(\lambda)
too.

Now we move on the non-refutability. Due to the persistency property of the blockchain, once a transaction/message goes more than
v
blocks deep into the blockchain of one honest player (where
v
is a security parameter), then it will be included in every honest player’s blockchain with overwhelming probability, and it will be assigned a permanent
position in the blockchain (so it will not be modified with an overwhelming probability). Also, due to the liveness property, all transactions originating from honest parties will eventually end up at a depth of more than
v
blocks in an honest player’s blockchain; therefore, the adversary cannot
perform a selective denial of service attack against honest account holders. Moreover, due to the security of the digital signature (i.e., existentially unforgeable under chosen message attacks), one cannot deny sending the messages it sent to the blockchain and smart contract.

\Box

5.3
\mathtt{ZSPA}
’s Extension:
\mathtt{ZSPA}
with an External Auditor (
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
)

In this section, we present an extension of
\mathtt{ZSPA}
, called
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
which lets a (trusted) third-party auditor,
{Aud}
, help identify misbehaving clients in the
\mathtt{ZSPA}
and generate a vector of random polynomials with a specific structure. Specifically, the functionality that
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
computes is defined as

f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}(\underbrace{\bot,...,\bot}_{\scriptscriptstyle m},b,\bm{\zeta})\rightarrow(\underbrace{(k,g,q),...,(k,g,q)}_{\scriptscriptstyle m},

\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},...,\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(m)})
, where
g
is the Markle tree’s root built on the pseudorandom values
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
,
q
is the hash value of the key used to generate the pseudorandom values,
m\geq 2
,
b
is constant value,
\bm{\zeta}
is a random polynomial of degree
1
, each
\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
has the form
\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}-\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
,
\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
is a random polynomial of degree
b-2
, and
\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle b-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
.

Informally,
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
requires that misbehaving parties are always detected, except with a negligible probability.
{Aud}
of this protocol will be invoked by Justitia when Justitia’s smart contract detects that a combination of the messages sent by the clients is not well-formed. Later, in Justitia’s proof, we will show that even a
semi-honest

{Aud}
who observes all messages that clients send to Justitia’s smart contracts, cannot learn anything about their set elements. We present
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
in Figure
3
.

\bullet

Parties. A set of clients
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
and an external auditor,
{Aud}
.

\bullet

Input.
m
: the total number of participants (excluding the auditor),
\bm{\zeta}
: a random polynomial of degree
1
,
b
: the total number of vectors, and
adr
: a deployed smart contract’s address. Let
b^{\prime}=b-1
.

\bullet

Output of each
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
.
k
: a secret key that generates
b
vectors
[z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,m}],...,[z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},m}]
of pseudorandom values,
h
: hash of the key,
g
: a Merkle tree’s root, and a vector of signed messages.

\bullet

Output of
{Aud}
.
L
: a list of misbehaving parties’ indices, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\mu}\hfil

: a vector of random polynomials.

1.

\mathtt{ZSPA}
invocation.

\mathtt{ZSPA}(\bot,...,\bot)\rightarrow\Big((k,g,q),...,(k,g,q)\Big)
.

All parties in
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
call the same instance of
\mathtt{ZSPA}
, which results in
(k,g,q),...,(k,g,q)
.

2.

Auditor computation.

\mathtt{Audit}(m,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

{Aud}
takes the below steps. Note, each
k_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern
