0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}
is given by
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. An honest party’s input,
k_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, equals
k
, where
1\leq j\leq m
.

(a)

runs the checks in the verification phase (i.e., Phase
3
) of
\mathtt{ZSPA}
for every
j
, i.e.,
\mathtt{Verify}(k_{\scriptscriptstyle j},g,q,m,auditor)\rightarrow(a_{\scriptscriptstyle j},s)
.

(b)

appends
j
to
L
, if any checks fails, i.e., if
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0
. In this case, it skips the next two steps for the current
j
.

(c)

For every
i
(where
0\leq i\leq b^{\prime}
), it recomputes
m
pseudorandom values:

\forall j,1\leq j\leq m-1:z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{PRF}(k,i||j),\hskip 11.38109ptz_{\scriptscriptstyle i,m}=-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
.

(d)

generates polynomial
\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
as follows:

\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}-\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
,
where
\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
is a random polynomial of degree
b^{\prime}-1
and
\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. By the end of this step, a vector

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\mu}\hfil

containing at most
m
polynomials is generated.

(e)

returns list
L
and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\mu}\hfil

.

Figure 3:
\mathtt{ZSPA}
with an external auditor (
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
)

Theorem 5.5

If
\mathtt{ZSPA}
is secure,
\mathtt{H}
is second-preimage resistant, and the correctness of
\mathtt{PRF}
,
\mathtt{H}
, and Merkle tree holds, then
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
securely computes
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}
in the presence of
m-1
malicious adversaries.

Proof

First, we consider the case where a sender, who (may collude with
m-2
senders and) generates pairs
(g,q)
, is corrupt.

Case 1: Corrupt sender.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
be the simulator using a subroutine adversary,
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
. Below, we explain how
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
works.

1.

simulates
\mathtt{CT}
and receives the output value
k
from
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
.

2.

sends
k
to TTP and receives back from it
m
pairs, where each pair is of the form
(g,q)
.

3.

sends
k
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
and receives back from it
m
pairs where each pair is of the form
(g^{\prime},q^{\prime})
.

4.

constructs an empty vector
L
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
checks whether the following equations hold for each
j
-th pair:
g=g^{\prime}\hskip 5.69054pt\wedge\hskip 5.69054ptq=q^{\prime}
. If these two equations do not hold, it sends an abort message
\Lambda
to other receiver clients, appends the index of the pair (i.e.,
j
) to
L
, and proceeds to the next step for the valid pairs. In the case where there are no valid pairs, it moves on to step
9
.

5.

picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
of degree
1
. Moreover, for every
j\notin L
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
of degree
b^{\prime}-1
, where
1\leq j\leq m
.

6.

computes
m
pseudorandom values for every
i,j^{\prime}
, where
0\leq i\leq b^{\prime}
and
j^{\prime}\notin L
as follows.

\forall j^{\prime},1\leq j^{\prime}\leq m-1:z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{PRF}(k,i||j^{\prime})\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and }\hskip 14.22636ptz_{\scriptscriptstyle i,m}=-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle j^{\prime}=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}

7.

generates polynomial
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, for every
j\notin L
, as follows:

{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}-\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, where
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
.

8.

sends the above
{\bm{\zeta}}
,
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, and
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
to all parties (i.e.,
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
and the receivers), for every
j\notin L
.

9.

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
outputs.

Now, we focus on the adversary’s output. In the real model, the messages that the adversary receives include those messages it receives as the result of the ideal call to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
and (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
), where
j\notin L
and
1\leq j\leq m
. Those messages yielded from the ideal calls have identical distribution to the distribution of the messages in the ideal model, as
\mathtt{CT}
is secure. The distribution of each
\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
depends on the distribution of its components; namely,
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, and
\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. As we are in the
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
-hybrid model, the distributions of
\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
in the real model and
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
in the ideal model are identical, as they were derived from the output of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
. Furthermore, in the real model, each polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
and
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
has been picked uniformly at random and they are independent of the clients’ and the adversary’s inputs. The same arguments hold for (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
) in the ideal model. Therefore, (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
) in the real model and (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
) in the ideal model have identical distributions.

Next, we turn our attention to the receiver’s output. We will show that the output distributions of an honest receiver and the auditor in the ideal and real models are computationally indistinguishable. In the real model, each element of the pair
(g,p)
is the output of a deterministic function on the output of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
. We know the outputs of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
in the real and ideal models have an identical distribution, and so do the evaluations of deterministic functions (namely Merkle tree,
\mathtt{H}
, and
\mathtt{PRF}
) on them. Therefore, each pair
(g,q)
in the real model has an identical distribution to the pair
(g,q)
in the ideal model. For the same reasons, the honest receiver in the real model aborts with the same probability as
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle S}
does in the ideal model. The same argument holds for the arbiter’s output, as it performs the same checks that an honest receiver does. Thus, the distribution of the joint outputs of the adversary, honest receiver, and honest in the real and ideal models is computationally indistinguishable.

Case 2: Corrupt receiver.
Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
be the simulator that uses subroutine adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
. Below, we explain how
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
works.

1.

simulates
\mathtt{ZSPA}
and receives the
m
output pairs of the form
(k,g,q)
from
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
.

2.

sends
(k,g,q)
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
and receives
m
keys,
k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
1\leq j\leq m
.

3.

generates an empty vector
L
. Next, for every
j
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
computes
q^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
as
\mathtt{H}(k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})=q_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. It generates
g_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
as follows.

(a)

for every
i
(where
0\leq i\leq b^{\prime}
), generates
m
pseudorandom values as below.

\forall j,1\leq j^{\prime}\leq m-1:z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{PRF}(k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j},i||j^{\prime}),\hskip 14.22636ptz_{\scriptscriptstyle i,m}=-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m-1}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}

(b)

constructs a Merkel tree on top of all pseudorandom values,
\mathtt{MT.genTree}(z_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},...,z_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},m})\rightarrow g^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
.

4.

checks if the following equations hold for each
j
-th pair:

(k=k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\ \wedge\ (g=g^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\ \wedge\ (q=q^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
.

5.

If these equations do not hold for
j
-th value, it appends
j
to
L
and proceeds to the next step for the valid value. In the case where there is no valid value, it moves on to step
9
.

6.

picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
of degree
1
. Also, for every
j\notin L
, it picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
of degree
b^{\prime}-1
, where
1\leq j\leq m
.

7.

generates polynomial
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, for every
j\notin L
, as follows:

{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}-\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, where
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime}}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, and values
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
were generated in step
3a
.

8.

sends the above
{\bm{\zeta}}
,
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
, and
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
to
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
, for every
j\notin L
and
1\leq j\leq m
.

9.

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
outputs.

In the real model, the adversary receives two sets of messages, the first set includes the transcripts (including
k,g,q
) it receives when it makes an ideal call to
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
and the second set includes pairs
({\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)})
, for every
j\notin L
and
1\leq j\leq m
. Since we are in the
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
-hybrid model and (based on our assumption) there is at least one honest party participated in
\mathtt{ZSPA}
(i.e., there are at most
m-1
malicious participants of
\mathtt{ZSPA}
), the distribution of the messages it receives from
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA}}
in the real and ideal models is identical. Furthermore, as we discussed in Case 1, (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
) in the real model and (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)},{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(j)}
) in the ideal model have identical distribution. The honest sender’s output distribution in both models is identical, as the distribution of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
’s output (i.e.,
k
) in both models is identical.

Now we show that the probability that the auditor aborts in the ideal and real models are statistically close. In the ideal model,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
is given the ideal functionality’s output that includes key
k
. Therefore, it can check whether the key that
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
sends to it equals
k
, i.e.,
k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle?}}{{=}}k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. Thus, it aborts with the probability
1
. However, in the real model, an honest auditor is not given the output of
\mathtt{CT}
(say key
k
) and it can only check whether the key is consistent with the hash value
q
and the Merkle tree’s root
g
stored on the blockchain. This means the adversary can distinguish the two models if in the real model it sends a key
\ddot{k}
, such that
\ddot{k}\neq k
and still passes the checks. Specifically, it sends the invalid key
\ddot{k}
that can generate valid pair
(g,q)
, as follows:
\mathtt{H}(\ddot{k})=q
and
\mathtt{MT.genTree}(z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0,1},...,z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle b^{\prime},m})\rightarrow g
, where each
z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is derived from
\ddot{k}
using the same technique described in step
3
above. Nevertheless, this means that the adversary breaks the second preimage resistance property of
\mathtt{H}
; however,
\mathtt{H}
is the second-preimage resistance and the probability that the adversary succeeds in finding the second preimage is negligible in the security parameter, i.e.,
\epsilon(\lambda)
where
\lambda
is the hash function’s security parameter. Therefore, in the real model, the auditor aborts if an invalid key is provided with a probability
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
which is statically close to the probability that
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle R}
aborts in the same situation in the ideal model, i.e.,
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
vs
1
.
Hence, the distribution of the joint outputs of the adversary, honest sender, and honest auditor in the real and ideal models is indistinguishable.

\Box

5.4
Unforgeable Polynomials

In this section, we introduce the notion of “unforgeable polynomials”. Informally, an unforgeable polynomial has a secret factor. To ensure that an unforgeable polynomial has not been tampered with, a verifier can check whether the polynomial is divisible by the secret factor.

To turn an arbitrary polynomial
\bm{\pi}
of degree
d
into an unforgeable polynomial
\bm{\theta}
, one can (i) pick three secret random polynomials
(\bm{\zeta},\bm{\omega},\bm{\gamma})
and (ii) compute
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{\pi}+\bm{\gamma}\bmod p
, where
deg(\bm{\zeta})=1,deg(\bm{\omega})=d,
and
deg(\bm{\gamma})=2d+1
.

To verify whether
\bm{\theta}
has been tampered with, a verifier (given
\bm{\theta},\bm{\gamma}
, and
\bm{\zeta}
) can check if
\bm{\theta}-\bm{\gamma}
is divisible by
\bm{\zeta}
. Informally, the security of an unforgeable polynomial states that an adversary (who does not know the three secret random polynomials) cannot tamper with an unforgeable polynomial without being detected, except with a negligible probability, in the security parameter. Below, we formally state it.

Theorem 5.6
(Unforgeable Polynomial)

Let polynomials
\bm{\zeta}
,
\bm{\omega}
, and
\bm{\gamma}
be three secret uniformly random polynomials (i.e.,
\bm{\zeta},\bm{\omega},\bm{\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[x]
), polynomial
\bm{\pi}
be an arbitrary polynomial,
deg(\bm{\zeta})=1,deg(\bm{\omega})=d,deg(\bm{\gamma})=2d+1
,
deg(\bm{\pi})=d
, and
p
be a
\lambda
-bit prime number. Also, let polynomial
\bm{\theta}
be defined as
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{\pi}+\bm{\gamma}\bmod p
. Given
(\bm{\theta},\bm{\pi})
, the probability that an adversary (which does not know
\bm{\zeta},\bm{\omega}
, and
\bm{\gamma}
) can forge
\bm{\theta}
to an arbitrary polynomial
\bm{\delta}
such that
\bm{\delta}\neq\bm{\theta}
,
deg(\bm{\delta})=const(\lambda)
, and
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\delta}-\bm{\gamma}
is negligible in the security parameter
\lambda
, i.e.,

Pr[\bm{\zeta}\ |\ (\bm{\delta}-\bm{\gamma})]\leq\epsilon(\lambda)

Proof

Let
\bm{\tau}=\bm{\delta}-\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\zeta}=a\cdot x+b
. Since
\bm{\gamma}
is a random polynomial of degree
2d+1
and unknown to the adversary, given
(\bm{\theta},\bm{\pi})
, the adversary cannot learn anything about the factor
\bm{\zeta}
; as from its point of view every polynomial of degree
1
in
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
is equally likely to be
\bm{\zeta}
. Moreover, polynomial
\bm{\tau}
has at most
Max\big(deg(\bm{\delta}),2d+1\big)
irreducible non-constant factors. For
\bm{\zeta}
to divide
\bm{\tau}
, one of the factors of
\bm{\tau}
must be equal to
\bm{\zeta}
. We also know that
\bm{\zeta}
has been picked uniformly at random (i.e.,
a,b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
). Thus, the probability that
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\tau}
is negligible in the security parameter,
\lambda
. Specifically,

Pr[\bm{\zeta}\ |\ (\bm{\delta}-\bm{\gamma})]\leq\frac{Max\big(deg(\bm{\delta}),2d+1\big)}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda}}=\epsilon(\lambda)

\Box

An interesting feature of an unforgeable polynomial is that the verifier can perform the check without needing to know the original polynomial
\bm{\pi}
. Another appealing feature of the unforgeable polynomial is that it supports
linear combination
and accordingly
batch verification
. Specifically, to turn
n
arbitrary polynomials
[\bm{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\bm{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle n}]
into unforgeable polynomials, one can construct
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot\bm{\pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\bmod p
, where
\forall i,1\leq i\leq n
.

To check whether all polynomials
[\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle n}]
are intact, a verifier can (i) compute their sum
\bm{\chi}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and (ii) check whether
\bm{\chi}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is divisible by
\bm{\zeta}
. Informally, the security of an unforgeable polynomial states that an adversary (who does not know the three secret random polynomials for each
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
) cannot tamper with any subset of the unforgeable polynomials without being detected, except with a negligible probability. We formally state it, below.

Theorem 5.7
(Unforgeable Polynomials’ Linear Combination)

Let polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
be a secret polynomial picked uniformly at random; also, let

, the probability that an adversary (which does not know

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\omega}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\bm{\gamma}\hfil

) can forge
t
polynomials, without loss of generality, say
\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}
to arbitrary polynomials
\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
such that
\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\neq\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
,
deg(\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})=const(\lambda)
, and
\bm{\zeta}
divides
(\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=t+1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
is negligible in the security parameter
\lambda
, i.e.,

Pr[\bm{\zeta}\ |\ (\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=t+1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})]\leq\epsilon(\lambda)

Proof

This proof is a generalisation of that of Theorem
5.6
.
Let
\bm{\tau}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and
\bm{\zeta}=a\cdot x+b
. Since every
\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
is a random polynomial of degree
2d+1
and unknown to the adversary, given
(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\theta}\hfil$\crcr}}},\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\bm{\pi}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\bm{\pi}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\bm{\pi}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\bm{\pi}\hfil$\crcr}}})
, the adversary cannot learn anything about the factor
\bm{\zeta}
. Each polynomial
\bm{\tau}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
has at most
Max\big(deg(\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}),2d+1\big)
irreducible non-constant factors.
We know that
\bm{\zeta}
has been picked uniformly at random (i.e.,
a,b\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
). Therefore, the probability that
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=t+1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
equals the probability that
\bm{\zeta}
equals to one of the factors of every
\bm{\tau}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, that is negligible in the security parameter. Concretely,

Pr[\bm{\zeta}\ |\ (\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}+\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=t+1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\theta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}\bm{\gamma}_{\scriptscriptstyle j})]\leq\frac{\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle t}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}Max\big(deg(\bm{\delta}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}),2d+1\big)}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle\lambda t}}=\epsilon(\lambda)

\Box

It is not hard to see that, Theorem
5.7
is a generalisation of Theorem
5.6
. Briefly, in Justitia, we will use unforgeable polynomials (and their linear combinations) to allow a smart contract to efficiently check whether the polynomials that the clients send to it are intact, i.e., they are
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s outputs.

6
Justitia: A Concrete Construction of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}

6.1
Main Challenges to Overcome

We need to address several key challenges, to design an efficient scheme that realises
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
. Below, we outline these challenges.

Keeping Overall Complexities Low.

In general, in multi-party PSIs, each client needs to send messages to the rest of the clients and/or engage in secure computation with them, e.g., in
[
27
,
35
]
, which would result in communication and/or computation quadratic with the number of clients. To address this challenge, we (a) allow one of the clients as a dealer to interact with the rest of the clients,
5
5

5

This approach has similarity with the non-secure PSIs in
[
24
]
.
and (b) we use a smart contract, which acts as a bulletin board to which most messages are sent and also performs lightweight computation on the clients’ messages. The combination of these approaches will keep the overall communication and computation linear with the number of clients (and sets’ cardinality).

Securely Randomising Input Polynomials.

In multi-party PSIs that rely on the polynomial representation, it is essential that an input polynomial of a client be randomised by another client
[
3
]
. To do that securely and efficiently, we require the dealer and each client together to engage in an instance of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, which we developed in Section
5
.

Preserving the Privacy of Outgoing Messages.

Although the use of regular public smart contracts (e.g., Ethereum) will help keep overall complexity low, it introduces another challenge; namely, if clients do not protect the privacy of the messages they send to the smart contracts, then other clients (e.g., dealer) and non-participants of PSI (i.e., the public) can learn the clients’ set elements and/or the intersection. Because standard smart contracts do not automatically preserve messages’ privacy. To efficiently protect the privacy of each client’s messages (sent to the contracts) from the dealer, we require the clients (except the dealer) to engage in
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
which lets each of them generate a pseudorandom polynomial with which it can blind its message. To protect the privacy of the intersection from the public, we require all clients to run a coin-tossing protocol to agree on a blinding polynomial, with which the final result that encodes the intersection on the smart contract will be blinded.

Ensuring the Correctness of Subroutine Protocols’ Outputs.

In general, any MPC that must remain secure against an active adversary is equipped with a verification mechanism that ensures an adversary is detected if it deviates from the protocol and affects messages’ integrity, during the protocol’s execution. This is the case for the subroutine protocols that we use, i.e.,
\mathtt{VOPR}
and
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
. Nevertheless, this type of verification itself is not always sufficient. Because in certain cases, the output of an MPC protocol may be fed as input to another MPC and we need to ensure that the
actual/intact
output of the first MPC is fed to the second one. This is the case in our PSI’s subroutines as well. To address this challenge, we use unforgeable polynomials; specifically, the output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
is an unforgeable polynomial (that encodes the actual output); if the adversary tampers with the
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s output and uses it later, then a verifier can detect it. We will have the same integrity guarantee for the output of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
for free. Because (i)
\mathtt{VOPR}
is called before
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, and (ii) if clients use intact outputs of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, then the final result (i.e., the sum of all clients’ messages) will not contain any output of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, as they would cancel out each other. Thus, by checking the correctness of the final result, one can ensure the correctness of the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
and
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, in one go.

6.2
Description of Justitia (
\mathtt{JUS}
)

An overview.

At a high level, Justitia (
\mathtt{JUS}
) works as follows. First, each client encodes its set elements into a polynomial. All clients sign a smart contract and deposit a predefined amount of coins into it. Next, one of the clients as a dealer,
D
, randomises the rest of the clients’ polynomials and imposes a certain structure to their polynomials. The clients also randomise
D
’s polynomials. The randomised polynomials reveal nothing about the clients’ original polynomials representing their set elements. Then, all clients send their randomised polynomials to the smart contract. The contract combines all polynomials and checks whether the resulting polynomial still has the structure imposed by
D
. If the contract concludes that the resulting polynomial does not have the structure, then it invokes an auditor,
{Aud}
, to identify misbehaving clients and penalise them. Nevertheless, if the resulting polynomial has the structure, then the contract outputs an encoded polynomial and refunds the clients’ deposits. In this case, all clients can use the resulting polynomial (output by the contract) to locally find the intersection. Figure
4
outlines the interaction between parties.

Figure 4:
Outline of the interactions between parties in Justitia

One of the novelties of
\mathtt{JUS}
is a lightweight verification mechanism which allows a smart contract to efficiently verify the correctness of the clients’ messages without being able to learn/reveal the clients’ set elements. To achieve this,
D
randomises each client’s polynomials and constructs unforgeable polynomials on the randomised polynomials (in one go). If any client modifies an unforgeable polynomial that it receives and sends the modified polynomial to the smart contract, then the smart contract would detect it, by checking whether the sum of all clients’ (unforgeable) polynomials is divisible by a certain polynomial of degree
1
. The verification is lightweight because: (i) it does not use any public key cryptography (often computationally expensive), (ii) it needs to perform only polynomial division, and (iii) it can perform batch verification, i.e., it sums all clients randomised polynomials and then checks the result’s correctness.

Now, we describe
\mathtt{JUS}
in more detail. First, all clients sign and deploy a smart contract,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. Each of them put a certain amount of deposit into it. Then, they together run
\mathtt{CT}
to agree on a key,
mk
, that will be used to generate a set of blinding polynomials to hide the final result from the public. Next, each client locally maps its set elements to a hash table and represents the content of each hash table’s bin as a polynomial,
\bm{\pi}
. After that, for each bin, the following steps are taken. All clients, except
D
, engage in
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
to agree on a set of pseudorandom blinding factors such that their sum is zero.

Then,
D
randomises and constructs an unforgeable polynomial on each client’s polynomial,
\bm{\pi}
. To do that,
D
and every other client engage in
\mathtt{VOPR}
that returns to the client a polynomial.
D
and every other client invoke
\mathtt{VOPR}
again to randomise
D
’s polynomial.
\mathtt{VOPR}
returns to the client another unforgeable polynomial. Note that the output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
reveals nothing about any client’s original polynomial
\bm{\pi}
, as this polynomial has been blinded/encrypted with another secret random polynomial by
D
, during the execution of
\mathtt{VOPR}
. Each client sums the two polynomials, blinds the result (using the output of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
), and sends it to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
.

After all of the clients send their input polynomials to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
,
D
sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
a
switching polynomial
that will allow
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
to obliviously switch the secret blinding polynomials used by
D
(during the execution of
\mathtt{VOPR}
) to blind each client’s original polynomial
\bm{\pi}
to another blinding polynomial that all clients can generate themselves, by using key
mk
. The switching polynomial is constructed in a way that does not affect the verification of unforgeable polynomials.

Next,
D
sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
a secret polynomial,
\bm{\zeta}
, that will allow
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
to check unforgeable polynomials’ correctness. Then,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
sums all clients’ polynomials and checks if
\bm{\zeta}
can divide the sum.
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
accepts the clients’ inputs if the polynomial divides the sum; otherwise, it invokes
{Aud}
to identify misbehaving parties. In this case, all honest parties’ deposit is refunded to them and the deposit of misbehaving parties is distributed among the honest ones as well. If all clients behave honestly, then each client can locally find the intersection. To do that, it uses
mk
to locally remove the blinding polynomial from the sum (that the contract generated), then evaluates the unblinded polynomial at each of its set elements and considers an element in the intersection when the evaluation equals zero.

Detailed Description of
\mathtt{JUS}
.

Below, we elaborate on how
\mathtt{JUS}
exactly works (see Table
1
for description of the main notations used).

1.

All clients in
{CL}=\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m},D\}
sign a smart contract:
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
and deploy it to a blockchain. All clients get the deployed contract’s address. Also, all clients engage in
\mathtt{CT}
to agree on a secret master key,
mk
.

2.

Each client in
{CL}
builds a hash table,
\mathtt{HT}
, and inserts the set elements into it, i.e.,
\forall i:\mathtt{H}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})={indx}
, then
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\rightarrow\mathtt{HT}_{\scriptscriptstyle indx}
. It pads every bin with random dummy elements to
d
elements (if needed). Then, for every bin, it constructs a polynomial whose roots are the bin’s content:
\bm{\pi}=\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle d}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
s^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is either
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
or a random value.

3.

Every client
C
in
{CL}\setminus D
, for every bin, agree on
b=3d+3
vectors of pseudorandom blinding factors:
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
, such that the sum of each vector elements is zero, i.e.,
\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=0
, where
0\leq i\leq b-1
. To do that, they participate in step
1
of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
. By the end of this step, for each bin, they agree on a secret key
k
(that will be used to generate the zero-sum values) as well as two values stored in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, i.e.,
q
: the key’s hash value and
g
: a Merkle tree’s root. After time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
,
D
ensures that all other clients have agreed on the vectors (i.e., all provided “approved” to the contract). If the check fails, it halts.

4.

Each client in
{CL}
deposits
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
amount to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. After time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, every client ensures that in total
(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})\cdot(m+1)
amount has been deposited. Otherwise, it halts and the clients’ deposit is refunded.

5.

D
picks a random polynomial
\bm{\zeta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
of degree
1
, for each bin.
It, for each client
C
, allocates to each bin two random polynomials:
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D,C)},\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D,C)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
of degree
d
, and two random polynomials:
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D,C)},\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D,C)}\leftarrow\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
of degree
3d+1
. Also, each client
C
, for each bin, picks two random polynomials:
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)},\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
of degree
d
, and ensures polynomials
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
do not contain zero coefficients.

6.

D
randomises other clients’ polynomials. To do so, for every bin, it invokes an instance of
\mathtt{VOPR}
(presented in Fig.
1
) with each client
C
; where
D
sends
\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
, while client
C
sends
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
to
\mathtt{VOPR}
. Each client
C
, for every bin, receives a blind polynomial of the following form:

\bm{\theta}^{{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}+\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}

from
\mathtt{VOPR}
. If any party aborts, the deposit would be refunded to all parties.

7.

Each client
{C}
randomises
{D}
’s polynomial. To do that, each client
{C}
, for each bin, invokes an instance of
\mathtt{VOPR}
with
{D}
, where each client
{C}
sends
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}
, while
{D}
sends
\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{{{(D)}}}
and
\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
to
\mathtt{VOPR}
. Every client
{C}
, for each bin, receives a blind polynomial of the following form:

\bm{\theta}^{{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}

from
\mathtt{VOPR}
. If any party aborts, the deposit would be refunded to all parties.

8.

Each client
C
, for every bin, masks the sum of polynomials
\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
using the blinding factors:
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}
, generated in step
3
. Specifically, it computes the following blind polynomial (for every bin):

\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}=\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}

where
\bm{\tau}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle 3d+2}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. Next, it sends all
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. If any party aborts, the deposit would be refunded to all parties.

9.

{D}
ensures all clients sent their inputs to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. If the check fails, it halts and the deposit would be refunded to all parties. It allocates a fresh pseudorandom polynomial
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
of degree
3d
, to each bin. To do so, it uses
mk
to derive a key for each bin:
k_{\scriptscriptstyle{{indx}}}=\mathtt{PRF}(mk,{{{indx}}})
and then uses the derived key to generate
3d+1
pseudorandom coefficients
g_{\scriptscriptstyle{{j,indx}}}=\mathtt{PRF}(k_{\scriptscriptstyle{{indx}}},j)
where
0\leq j\leq 3d
. Also, for each bin, it allocates a fresh random polynomial:
\bm{\omega}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
of degree
d
.

10.

{D}
, for every bin, computes a polynomial of the form:

\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})+\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}

It sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
polynomials
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
and
\bm{\zeta}
, for each bin.

11.

\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
takes the following steps:

(a)

for every bin, sums all related polynomials provided by all clients in
\bar{P}
:

\begin{split}\bm{\phi}&=\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}\\
&=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bigg(\bm{\omega}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(C)}})+\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{C=}{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}})+\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}\bigg)\end{split}

(b)

checks whether, for every bin,
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\phi}
. If the check passes, it sets
Flag=True
. Otherwise, it sets
Flag=False
.

12.

If the above check passes (i.e.,
Flag=True
), then the following steps are taken:

(a)

\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
sends back each party’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
amount.

(b)

each client (given
\bm{\zeta}
and
mk
) finds the elements in the intersection as follows.

i.

derives a bin’s pseudorandom polynomial,
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
, from
mk
.

ii.

removes the blinding polynomial from each bin’s polynomial:

\bm{\phi}^{\prime}=\bm{\phi}-\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}

iii.

evaluates each bin’s unblinded polynomial at every element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
belonging to that bin and considers the element in the intersection if the evaluation is zero: i.e.,
\bm{\phi}^{\prime}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
.

13.

If the check does not pass (i.e.,
Flag=False
), then the following steps are taken.

(a)

{Aud}
asks every client
{C}
to send to it the
\mathtt{PRF}
’s key (generated in step
3
), for every bin. It inserts the keys to

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle k\hfil

. It generates a list
\bar{L}
initially empty. Then, for every bin,
{Aud}
takes step
2
of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, i.e., invokes
\mathtt{Audit}(m,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{k}\hfil$\crcr}}},q,\bm{\zeta},3d+3,g)\rightarrow(L,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\mu}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\mu}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\mu}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\mu}\hfil$\crcr}}})
. Every time it invokes
\mathtt{Audit}
, it appends the elements of returned
L
to
\bar{L}
.
{Aud}
for each bin sends

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\mu}\hfil

to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. It also sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
the list
\bar{L}
of all misbehaving clients detected so far.

(b)

to help identify further misbehaving clients,
D
takes the following steps, for each bin of client
{C}
whose ID is not in
\bar{L}
.

i.

computes polynomial
\bm{\chi}^{{{(D,C)}}}
as follows.

\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}-(\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})

where
\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
is a fresh random polynomial of degree
3d+1
.

ii.

sends polynomial
\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
.

Note, if
\bar{L}
contains all clients’ IDs, then
D
does not need to take the above steps
13(b)i
and
13(b)ii
.

(c)

\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, takes the following steps to check if the client misbehaved, for each bin of client
{C}
whose ID is not in
\bar{L}
.

i.

computes polynomial
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
as follows:

\begin{split}\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}&=\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}+\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}\\
&=\bm{\zeta}\cdot(\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}+\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}})\end{split}

where
\bm{\mu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}
generated and sent to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
by
{Aud}
in step
13a
.

ii.

checks if
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
. If the check fails, it appends the client’s ID to a list
L^{\prime}
.

If
\bar{L}
contains all clients’ IDs, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
does not take the above two steps.

(d)

\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
refunds the honest parties’ deposit. Also, it retrieves the total amount of
\ddot{ch}
from the deposit of dishonest clients (i.e., those clients whose IDs are in
\bar{L}
or
L^{\prime}
) and sends it to
{Aud}
. It also splits the remaining deposit of the misbehaving parties among the honest ones. Thus, each honest client receives
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}+\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
amount in total, where
m^{\prime}
is the total number of misbehaving parties.

One may be tempted to replace
{Justitia}
with a scheme in which all clients send their encrypted sets to a server (potentially semi-honest and plays
{Aud}
’s role) which computes the result in a privacy-preserving manner. We highlight that the main difference is that in this (hypothetical) scheme the server is
always involved
; whereas, in our protocol,
{Aud}
remains offline as long as the clients behave honestly and it is invoked only when the contract detects misbehaviours.

In step
5
,
\mathtt{JUS}
requires each client
C
to ensure polynomials
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
do not contain zero coefficients. However, this check can be removed from this step, if we allow the protocol to output error with a negligible probability. We refer readers to Appendix
0.E
for further discussion.

Next, we present a theorem that formally states the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
.

Theorem 6.1

Let polynomials
\bm{\zeta}
,
\bm{\omega}
, and
\bm{\gamma}
be three secret uniformly random polynomials. If
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{\pi}+\bm{\gamma}\bmod p
is an unforgeable polynomial (w.r.t. Theorem
5.6
),
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
,
\mathtt{VOPR}
,
\mathtt{PRF}
, and smart contracts are secure, then
\mathtt{JUS}
securely realises
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with
Q
-fairness (w.r.t. Definition
7
) in the presence of
m-1
active-adversary clients (i.e.,
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s) or a passive dealer client, passive auditor, or passive public.

6.3
Proof of
\mathtt{JUS}

In this section, we prove Theorem
6.1
, i.e., the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
.

Proof

We prove Theorem
6.1
by considering the case where each party is corrupt, at a time.

Case 1: Corrupt
m-1
clients in
\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
. Let
P^{\prime}
be a set of at most
m-1
corrupt clients, where
P^{\prime}\subset\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
. Let set
\hat{P}
be defined as follows:
\hat{P}=\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}\setminus P^{\prime}
. Also, let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
be the simulator, which uses a subroutine adversary,
\mathcal{A}
. Below, we explain how
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
(which receives the input sets of honest dealer
D
and honest client(s) in
\hat{P}
) works.

1.

constructs and deploys a smart contract. It sends the contract’s address to
\mathcal{A}
.

2.

simulates
\mathtt{CT}
and receives the output value,
{mk}
, from its functionality,
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
.

3.

simulates
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
for each bin and receives the output value,
(k,g,q)
, from its functionality,
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}
.

4.

deposits in the contract the total amount of
(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})\cdot(m-|P^{\prime}|+1)
coins on behalf of
D
and honest client(s) in
\hat{P}
. It sends to
\mathcal{A}
the amount deposited in the contract.

5.

checks if
\mathcal{A}
has deposited
(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})\cdot|P^{\prime}|
amount. If the check fails, it instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited and sends message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP (and accordingly to all parties); it outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}
outputs and then halts.

6.

picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
of degree
1
, for each bin. Also,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
, for each client
{C}\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
allocates to each bin two random polynomials: (
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}},{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
) of degree
d
and two random polynomials: (
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
,
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
) of degree
3d+1
. Moreover,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
for every honest client
C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}
, for each bin, picks two random polynomials: (
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}
,
{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}
) of degree
d
.

7.

simulates
\mathtt{VOPR}
using inputs
{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
for each bin. Accordingly, it receives the inputs of clients
C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
, i.e.,
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, from its functionality
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}
, for each bin.

8.

extracts the roots of polynomial
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
for each bin and appends those roots that are in the sets universe to a new set
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime\prime})}
.

9.

simulates
\mathtt{VOPR}
again using inputs
{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
, for each bin.

10.

sends to TTP the input sets of all parties; namely, (i) client
D
’s input set:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
, (ii) honest clients’ input sets:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime})}
for all
C^{\prime}
in
\hat{P}
, and (iii)
\mathcal{A}
’s input sets:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime\prime})}
, for all
C^{\prime\prime}
in
P^{\prime}
. For each bin, it receives the intersection set,
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
, from TTP.

11.

represents the intersection set for each bin as a polynomial,
{\bm{\pi}}
, as follows:
{\bm{\pi}}=\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
.

12.

constructs polynomials
{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}},{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}
, and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}={\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
, for each bin and each honest client
C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}
, where
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle 3d+2}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and each
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}
is derived from
k
.

13.

sends to
\mathcal{A}
polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
for each bin and each client
C^{\prime}
.

14.

receives
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
from
\mathcal{A}
, for each bin and each client
C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
. It ensures that the output for every
C^{\prime\prime}
has been provided. Otherwise, it halts.

15.

if there is any abort within steps
7
–
14
, then it sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited. It outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}
outputs and then halts.

16.

constructs polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})+{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
for each bin, where
{\bm{\omega}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
is a fresh random polynomial of degree
d
and
{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
is a pseudorandom polynomial derived from
{mk}
.

17.

sends to
\mathcal{A}
polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
and
{\bm{\zeta}}
for each bin.

18.

given each
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, computes polynomial
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}
as follows
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}}{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}}({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}})
, for every bin. Then,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
checks whether
{\bm{\zeta}}
divides
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}
, for every bin. If the check passes, it sets
Flag=True
. Otherwise, it sets
Flag=False
.

19.

if
Flag=True
:

(a)

instructs the ledger to send back each party’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
amount. It sends a message
deliver
to TTP.

(b)

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}
outputs and then halts.

20.

if
Flag=False
:

(a)

receives
|P^{\prime}|
keys of the
\mathtt{PRF}
from
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern , for every bin.

checks whether the following equation holds:

, for every

. Note that

is the output of

generated in step

. It constructs an empty list
L^{\prime}
and appends to it the indices (e.g.,

) of the keys that do not pass the above check.

(c)

simulates
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
and receives from
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}
the output that contains a vector of random polynomials,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\prime}
, for each valid key.

(d)

sends to
\mathcal{A}
, the list
L^{\prime}
and vector
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern , for every bin.

(e)

for each bin of client
{C}
whose index (or ID) is not in
L^{\prime}
computes polynomial
{\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
as follows:

{\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}-({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})
, where
{\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
is a fresh random polynomial of degree
3d+1
. Note,
C
includes both honest and corrupt clients, except those clients whose index is in
L^{\prime}
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
sends every polynomial
{\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
to
\mathcal{A}
.

(f)

given each
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
(by
\mathcal{A}
in step
14
), computes polynomial
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
as follows:
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}={\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}-{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}-{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, for every bin. Then,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
checks whether
{\bm{\zeta}}
divides
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, for every bin. It appends the index of those clients that did not pass the above check to a new list,
L^{\prime\prime}
. Note that
L^{\prime}\cap L^{\prime\prime}=\bot
.

(g)

if
L^{\prime}
or
L^{\prime\prime}
is not empty, then it instructs the ledger: (i) to refund the coins of those parties whose index is not in
L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime\prime}
, (ii) to retrieve
\ddot{ch}
amount from the adversary (i.e., one of the parties whose index is in one of the lists) and send the
\ddot{ch}
amount to the auditor, and (iii) to compensate each honest party (whose index is not in the two lists)
\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
amount, where
m^{\prime}=|L^{\prime}|+|L^{\prime\prime}|
. Then, it sends message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
to TTP.

(h)

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}
outputs and halts.

Next, we show that the real and ideal models are computationally indistinguishable. We first focus on the adversary’s output. In the real and ideal models, the adversary sees the transcripts of ideal calls to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
as well as this functionality outputs, i.e.,
mk
. Due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
(as we are in the
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
-hybrid world), the transcripts of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
in both models have identical distribution, so have the random output of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
, i.e.,
mk
. The same holds for (the transcripts and) outputs (i.e.,
(k,g,q)
) of
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}
that the adversary observes in the two models. Also, the deposit amount is identical in both models. Thus, in the case where
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is disseminated at this point; the adversary’s output distribution in both models is identical.

The adversary also observes (the transcripts and) outputs of ideal calls to
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}
in both models, i.e., output (
\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}+\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}},\bm{\theta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}
) for each corrupted client
C^{\prime\prime}
. However, due to the security of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, the
\mathcal{A}
’s view, regarding
\mathtt{VOPR}
, in both models have identical distribution.
In the real model, the adversary observes the polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
that each honest client
C
stores in the smart contract. Nevertheless, this is a blinded polynomial comprising of two uniformly random blinding polynomials (i.e.,
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
) unknown to the adversary. In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
is given polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
for each honest client
C^{\prime}
. This polynomial has also been blinded via two uniformly random polynomials (i.e.,
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}
) unknown to
\mathcal{A}
. Thus,
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
in the real model and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
in the ideal model have identical distributions. As a result, in the case where
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
is disseminated at this point; the adversary’s output distribution in both models is identical.

Furthermore, in the real world, the adversary observes polynomials
{\bm{\zeta}}
and
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
that
D
stores in the smart contract. Nevertheless,
{\bm{\zeta}}
is a uniformly random polynomial, also polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
has been blinded; its blinding factors are the additive inverse of the sum of the random polynomials
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
unknown to the adversary, for every client
C\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
and
D
. In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
is given
{\bm{\zeta}}
and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
, where the former is a random polynomial and the latter is a blinded polynomial that has been blinded with the additive inverse of the sum of random polynomials
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
unknown to it, for all client
C
. Therefore, (
{\bm{\zeta}},\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
) in the real model and (
{\bm{\zeta}},{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
) in the ideal model component-wise have identical distribution.

Also, the sum of less than
m+1
blinded polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle m})},\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
in the real model has identical distribution to the sum of less than
m+1
blinded polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle m})},{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
in the ideal model, as such a combination would still be blinded by a set of random blinding polynomials unknown to the adversary. Now we discuss why the two polynomials
\frac{\bm{\phi}}{\bm{\zeta}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
in the real model and
\frac{{\bm{\phi}}}{{\bm{\zeta}}}-{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
in the ideal model are indistinguishable. Note that we divide and then subtract polynomials
{\bm{\phi}}
because the adversary already knows (and must know) polynomials
(\bm{\zeta},\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
. In the real model, polynomial
\frac{\bm{\phi}}{\bm{\zeta}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
has the following form:

\frac{\bm{\phi}}{\bm{\zeta}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\bm{\omega}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(C)}})+\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}})=\bm{\mu}\cdot gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})

(2)

In Equation
2
, every element of
[\bm{\omega}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},...,\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}},\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}]
is a uniformly random polynomial for every client
C\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
(including corrupt ones) and client
D
; because it has been picked by (in this case honest) client
D
. Thus, as shown in Section
3.9
,
\frac{\bm{\phi}}{\bm{\zeta}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
has the form
\bm{\mu}\cdot gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
, where
\bm{\mu}
is a uniformly random polynomial and
gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
represents the intersection of the input sets.

In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
can construct polynomial
{\bm{\phi}}
using its (well-formed) inputs
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
and polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
that the simulator has sent to it, for all
C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}
and all
C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
. Thus, in the ideal model, polynomial
\frac{{\bm{\phi}}}{\bm{\zeta}}-{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
has the following form:

\begin{split}\frac{{\bm{\phi}}}{\bm{\zeta}}-{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}=&\ {\bm{\pi}}\cdot\Big(\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}}(\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}})+\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}}(\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}})\Big)+\\
+&\Big(\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}}(\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(C^{\prime\prime})}})+\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}}(\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}})\Big)\\
=&\ {\bm{\mu}}\cdot gcd({\bm{\pi}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}})\end{split}

(3)

In Equation
3
, every element of the vector
[\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}},\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}},\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}},\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}]
is a uniformly random polynomial for all
C^{\prime}\in\hat{P}
and all
C^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
, as they have been picked by
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
. Therefore,
\frac{{\bm{\phi}}}{\bm{\zeta}}-{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
equals
