{\bm{\mu}}\cdot gcd({\bm{\pi}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}})
, such that
{\bm{\mu}}
is a uniformly random polynomial and
gcd({\bm{\pi}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}})
represents the intersection of the input sets. We know that
gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})=gcd({\bm{\pi}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}})
, as
{\bm{\pi}}
includes the intersection of all clients’ sets. Also,
{\bm{\mu}}
has identical distribution in the two models, because they are uniformly random polynomials. Thus,
\frac{\bm{\phi}}{\bm{\zeta}}-\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
in the real model and
\frac{{\bm{\phi}}}{{\bm{\zeta}}}-{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
in the ideal model are indistinguishable.

Now we focus on the case where
Flag=False
. In the real model, the adversary observes the output of
\mathtt{Audit}(.)
which is a list of indices
L
and a vector of random polynomials

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil

picked by an honest auditor. In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
is given a list
L^{\prime}
of indices and a vector of random polynomials
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\prime}
picked by the simulator. Thus, the pair (
L,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}
) in the real model has identical distribution to the pair (
L^{\prime},\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\prime}
) in the ideal model. Moreover, in the real model, the adversary observes each polynomial
\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}-(\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})
that
D
stores in the contract, for each bin and each client
C
whose index is not in
L
. This is a blinded polynomial with blinding factor
\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
which itself is a uniformly random polynomial picked by
D
. In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
is given a polynomial of the form
{\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}-({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})
, for each bin and each client
C
whose index is not in
L^{\prime}
. This is also a blinded polynomial whose blinding factor is
{\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
which itself is a random polynomial picked by the simulator. Therefore,
\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
in the real model has identical distribution to
\bm{\chi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
in the ideal model. In the real model, the adversary observes polynomial
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}=\bm{\zeta}\cdot(\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}+\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}+\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}})
which is a blinded polynomial whose blinding factor is the sum of the above random polynomials, i.e.,
\bm{\eta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
. In the ideal model,
\mathcal{A}
already has polynomials
{\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}},{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
, and
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
, where
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}\in{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mu\hfil$\crcr}}}^{\prime}}
; this lets
\mathcal{A}
compute

{\bm{\iota}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}={\bm{\chi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}+{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot({\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}})
,
where
{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
is a random blinding polynomial used in
{\bm{\mu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
. Nevertheless,
{\bm{\iota}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
itself is a blinded polynomial whose blinding factor is the sum of random polynomials, i.e.,
{\bm{\eta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\xi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
. Hence, the distribution of polynomial
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
in the real model and
{\bm{\iota}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
in the ideal model are identical. Moreover, the integer
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}+\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
has identical distribution in both models.

Next, we show that the honest party aborts with the same probability in the real and ideal models. Due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
, an honest party (during the execution of
\mathtt{CT}
) aborts with the same probability in both models; in this case, the adversary learns nothing about the parties’ input set and the sets’ intersection as the parties have not sent out any encoded input set yet. The same holds for the probability that honest parties abort during the execution of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
. In this case, an aborting adversary also learns nothing about the parties’ input set and the sets’ intersection. Since all parties’ deposit is public, an honest party can look up and detect if not all parties have deposited a sufficient amount with the same probability in both models. If parties halt because of insufficient deposit, no one learns about the parties’ input set and the sets’ intersection because the inputs (representation) have not been sent out at this point.

Due to the security of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, honest parties abort with the same probability in both models. In the case of an abort during
\mathtt{VOPR}
execution, the adversary would learn nothing (i) about its counter party’ input set due to the security of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, and (ii) about the rest of the honest parties’ input sets and the intersection as the other parties’ input sets are still blinded by random blinding factors known only to
D
. In the real model,
D
can check if all parties provided their encoded inputs, by reading from the smart contract. The simulator also can do the same check to ensure
\mathcal{A}
has provided the encoded inputs of all corrupt parties. Therefore, in both models, an honest party with the same probability would detect the violation of such a requirement, i.e., providing all encoded inputs. Even in this case, if an adversary aborts (by not proving its encoded inputs), then it learns nothing about the honest parties’ input sets and the intersection for the reason explained above.

In the real model, the smart contract sums every client
C
’s polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
with each other and with
D
’s polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
, which removes the blinding factors that
D
initially inserted (during the execution of
\mathtt{VOPR}
), and then checks whether the result is divisible by
\bm{\zeta}
. Due to (a) Theorem
5.7
(i.e., unforgeable polynomials’ linear combination), (b) the fact that the smart contract is given the random polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
in plaintext, (c) no party (except honest client
D
) knew anything about
\bm{\zeta}
before they send their input to the contract, and (d) the security of the contract (i.e., the adversary cannot influence the correctness of the above verification performed by the contract), the contract can detect if a set of outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
has been tampered with, with a probability at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
. In the ideal model,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
also can remove the blinding factors and it knows the random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
, unlike the adversary who does not know
{\bm{\zeta}}
when it sends the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
to
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
. So,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can detect when
\mathcal{A}
modifies a set of the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
that were sent to
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
with a probability at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
, due to Theorem
5.7
. Hence, the smart contract in the real model and the simulator in the ideal model would abort with a similar probability.

Moreover, due to the security of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, the probability that an invalid key
k_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle k\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle k\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle k\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle k\hfil$\crcr}}}
is added to the list
L
in the real world is similar to the probability that
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
detects an invalid key
k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}
in the ideal world. In the real model, when
Flag=False
, the smart contract can identify each ill-structured output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
(i.e.,
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
) with a probability of at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
by checking whether
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
, due to (a) Theorem
5.6
(i.e., unforgeable polynomial), (b) the fact that the smart contract is given
\bm{\zeta}
in plaintext, (c) no party (except honest client
D
) knew anything about
\bm{\zeta}
before they send their input to the contract, and (d) the security of the contract. In the ideal model, when
Flag=False
, given each
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can remove its blinding factors from
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
which results in
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
and then check if
{\bm{\zeta}}
divides
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
. The simulator can also detect an ill-structured
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
with a probability of at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
, due to Theorem
5.6
, the fact that the simulator is given
{\bm{\zeta}}
in plaintext, and the adversary is not given any knowledge about
{\bm{\zeta}}
before it sends to the simulator the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
. Hence, the smart contract in the real model and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
in the ideal model would detect an ill-structured input of an adversary with the same probability.

Now, we analyse the output of the predicates
(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
in the real and ideal models. In the real model, all clients proceed to prepare their input set only if the predefined amount of coins has been deposited by the parties; otherwise, they will be refunded and the protocol halts. In the ideal model also the simulator proceeds to prepare its inputs only if a sufficient amount of deposit has been put in the contract; otherwise, it would send message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP. Thus, in both models, the parties proceed to prepare their inputs only if
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}(.)\rightarrow 1
.
In the real model, if there is an abort after the parties ensure there is enough deposit and before client
D
provides its encoded input to the contract, then all parties would be able to retrieve their deposit in full; in this case, the aborting adversary would not be able to learn anything about honest parties input sets, because the parties’ input sets are still blinded by random blinding polynomials known only to client
D
. In the ideal model, if there is any abort during steps
7
–
14
, then the simulator sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited. Also, in the case of an abort (within the above two points of time), the auditor is not involved. Thus, in both models, in the case of an abort within the above points of time, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}(.)\rightarrow 1
. In the real model, if
Flag=True
, then all parties would be able to learn the intersection and the smart contract refunds all parties, i.e., sends each party
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
amount which is the amount each party initially deposited.

In the ideal model, when
Flag=True
, then
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can extract the intersection (by summing the output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
provided by all parties and removing the blinding polynomials) and sends back each party’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
amount. Hence, in both models in the case of
Flag=True
, when all of the parties receive the result, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}(.)\rightarrow 1
. In the real model, when
Flag=False
, only the adversary which might corrupt
m^{\prime}
clients would be able to learn the result; in this case, the contract sends (i)
\ddot{ch}
amount to the auditor, and (ii)
\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
amount as a compensation, to each honest party, in addition to each party’s deposit
\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}
. In the ideal model, when
Flag=False
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to distribute the same amount among the auditor (e.g., with address
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
) and every honest party (e.g., with address
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
) as the contract does in the real model. Thus, in both models when
Flag=False
, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}(.,.,.,.,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\rightarrow(a=1,.)
and
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}(.,.,.,.,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\rightarrow(.,b=1)
.

We conclude that the distributions of the joint outputs of the honest client
C\in\hat{P}
, client
D
,
{Aud}
, and the adversary in the real and ideal models are computationally indistinguishable.

Case 2: Corrupt dealer
D
. In the real execution, the dealer’s view is defined as follows:

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}\Big(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},...,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(m)})\Big)=

\{S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},m\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}),r_{\scriptscriptstyle D},\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D},k,g,q,\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D},\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}\}

where
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
refer to the dealer’s real-model view during the execution of
\mathtt{CT}
and
\mathtt{VOPR}
respectively. Also,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
is the outcome of internal random coins of client
D
and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
is the address of contract
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. The simulator
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
, which receives all parties’ input sets, works as follows.

1.

receives from the subroutine adversary polynomials
{\bm{\zeta}},({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})}),...,({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
,
({\bm{\omega}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})}),...,

({\bm{\omega}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
, where
deg({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)})=deg({\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)})=3d+1,deg({\bm{\omega}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)})=deg({\bm{\rho}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)})=d
, and
deg({\bm{\zeta}})=1
, where
C\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
.

2.

generates an empty view. It appends to the view, the input set
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
. It constructs and deploys a smart contract. Let
{adr}_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
be the contract’s address. It appends
{adr}_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
to the view.

3.

appends to the view integer
m\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})
and coins
r^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
chosen uniformly at random.

4.

extracts the simulation of
\mathtt{CT}
from
\mathtt{CT}
’s simulator for client
D
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
be the simulation. It appends
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
to the view.

5.

picks a random key,
k^{\prime}
, and derives pseudorandom values
z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
from the key (the same way is done in Figure
2
). It constructs a Merkle tree on top of all values
z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
. Let
g^{\prime}
be the root of the resulting tree. It appends
k^{\prime},g^{\prime}
, and
q^{\prime}=\mathtt{H}(k^{\prime})
to the view.

6.

invokes
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s functionality twice and extracts the simulation of
\mathtt{VOPR}
from
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s simulator for client
D
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
be the simulation. It appends
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
to the view.

7.

given the parties’ input sets, computes a polynomial
{\bm{\pi}}
that represents the intersection of the sets.

8.

picks
m
random polynomials
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})}
of degree
3d+1
such that their sum is
0
.

9.

picks
m
pairs of random polynomials
({\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})}),...,({\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
, where each polynomial is of degree
d
. Then,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
for each client
C\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
computes polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}\cdot({\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)}+{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)})+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}+{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}+{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
.

10.

appends
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})}
and the intersection of the sets
S^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
to the view.

Next, we will show that the two views are computationally indistinguishable.
D
’s input
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
is
identical in both models; therefore, they have identical distributions. Also, the contract’s address has the same distribution in both views, and so has the integer
m\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})
. Since the real-model semi-honest adversary samples its randomness according to the protocol’s description, the random coins in both models (i.e.,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
r^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
) have identical distributions. Moreover, due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
,
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
have identical distributions. Keys
k
and
k^{\prime}
have identical distributions, as both have been picked uniformly at random from the same domain. In the real model, each element of the pair
(g,p)
is the output of a deterministic function on a random value
k
. We know that
k
in the real model has identical distribution to
k^{\prime}
in the ideal model, so do the evaluations of deterministic functions (i.e., Merkle tree,
\mathtt{H}
, and
\mathtt{PRF}
) on them. Therefore, each pair
(g,q)
in the real model component-wise has an identical distribution to each pair
(g^{\prime},q^{\prime})
in the ideal model.
Furthermore, due to the security of the
\mathtt{VOPR}
,
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
have identical distributions.

In the real model, each
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
has been blinded by a pseudorandom polynomial (i.e., derived from
\mathtt{PRF}
’s output) unknown to client
D
. In the ideal model, however, each
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
has been blinded by a random polynomial unknown to client
D
. Due to the security of
\mathtt{PRF}
, its outputs are computationally indistinguishable from truly random values. Therefore,
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
in the real model and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
in the ideal model are computationally indistinguishable. Now we focus on the sum of all
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
in the real model and the sum of all
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
in the ideal model, as adding them together would remove the above blinding polynomials that are unknown to client
D
. Specifically, in the real model, after client
D
sums all
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
and removes the blinding factors and
\bm{\zeta}
that it initially imposed, it would get a polynomial of the form
\bm{\hat{\phi}}=\frac{\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})}{\bm{\zeta}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(C)}})+\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}(\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}})
, where
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C,D)}}}
are random polynomials unknown to client
D
.
In the ideal model, after summing all
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
and removing the random polynomials that it already knows, it would get a polynomial of the following form:

\bm{\hat{\phi}}^{\prime}=\frac{\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)})}{{\bm{\zeta}}}={\bm{\pi}}\cdot\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}({\bm{\omega}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)})+({\bm{\rho}}^{\prime\scriptscriptstyle(C)}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)})
.

As shown in Section
3.9
, polynomial
\bm{\hat{\phi}}
has the form
\bm{\mu}\cdot gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
, where
\bm{\mu}
is a uniformly random polynomial and
gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
represents the intersection of the input sets. Moreover, it is evident that
\bm{\hat{\phi}}^{\prime}
has the form
{\bm{\mu}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}
, where
{\bm{\mu}}
is a random polynomial and
{\bm{\pi}}
represents the intersection. We know that both
gcd(\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}},\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
and
{\bm{\pi}}
represent the same intersection, also
{\bm{\mu}}
in the real model and
{\bm{\mu}}
in the ideal model have identical distribution as they are uniformly random polynomials. Thus, two polynomials
\bm{\hat{\phi}}
and
\bm{\hat{\phi}}^{\prime}
are indistinguishable. Also, the output
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
is identical in both views. We conclude that the two views are computationally indistinguishable.

Case 3: Corrupt auditor
. In this case, by using the proof that we have already provided for Case 1 (i.e.,
m-1
client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s are corrupt), we can easily construct a simulator that generates a view computationally distinguishable from the real-model semi-honest auditor.
The reason is that, in the worst-case scenario where
m-1
malicious client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s reveal their input sets and randomness to the auditor, the auditor’s view would be similar to the view of these corrupt clients, which we have shown to be indistinguishable. The only extra messages the auditor generates, that a corrupt client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
would not see in plaintext, are random blinding polynomials
(\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\xi}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})
generated during the execution of
\mathtt{Audit}(.)
of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
; however, these polynomials are picked uniformly at random and independent of the parties’ input sets. Thus, if the smart contract detects misbehaviour and invokes the auditor, even if
m-1
corrupt client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
reveals their input sets, then the auditor cannot learn anything about honest parties’ input sets.

Case 4: Corrupt public
. In the real model, the view of the public (i.e., non-participants of the protocol) is defined as below:

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle Pub}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}\Big(\bot,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})\Big)=

\{\bot,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},(m+1)\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch}),k,g,q,\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}\}

Now, we describe how the simulator
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Pub}
works.

1.

generates an empty view and appends to it an empty symbol,
\bot
. It constructs and deploys a smart contract. It appends the contract’s address,
{adr}_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
and integer
(m+1)\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})
to the view.

2.

picks a random key,
k^{\prime}
, and derives pseudorandom values
z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
from the key, in the same way, done in Figure
2
. It constructs a Merkle tree on top of the
z^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
values. Let
g^{\prime}
be the root of the resulting tree. It appends
k^{\prime},g^{\prime}
, and
q^{\prime}=\mathtt{H}(k^{\prime})
to the view.

3.

for each client
C\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
and client
D
generates a random polynomial of degree
3d+1
(for each bin), i.e.,
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
.

Next, we will show that the two views are computationally indistinguishable. In both views,
\bot
is identical. Also, the contract’s addresses (i.e.,
{adr}_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
) has the same
distribution in both views, and so has the integer
(m+1)\cdot(\ddot{y}+\ddot{ch})
. Keys
k
and
k^{\prime}
have identical distributions as well, because both of them have been picked uniformly at random from the same domain. In the real model, each element of pair
(g,p)
is the output of a deterministic function on the random key
k
. We know that
k
in the real model has identical distribution to
k^{\prime}
in the ideal model, and so do the evaluations of deterministic functions on them. Hence, each pair
(g,q)
in the real model component-wise has an identical distribution to each pair
(g^{\prime},q^{\prime})
in the ideal model. In the real model, each polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
is a blinded polynomial comprising of two uniformly random blinding polynomials (i.e.,
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
) unknown to the adversary. In the ideal model, each polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C)}
is a random polynomial; thus, polynomials
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})}
in the real model have identical distribution to polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})}
in the ideal model. Similarly, polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
has been blinded in the real model; its blinding factors are the additive inverse of the sum of the random polynomials
\bm{\gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
\bm{\delta}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
unknown to the adversary. In the ideal model, polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
is a uniformly random polynomial; thus,
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
in the real model and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
in the ideal model have identical distributions.
Moreover, in the real model even though the sum
\bm{\phi}
of polynomials
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
would remove some of the blinding random polynomials, it is still a blinded polynomial with a pseudorandom blinding factor
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
(derived from the output of
\mathtt{PRF}
), unknown to the adversary. In the ideal model, the sum of polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})},{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
is also a random polynomial. Thus, the sum of the above polynomials in the real model is computationally indistinguishable from the sum of those polynomials in the ideal model. We conclude that the two views are computationally indistinguishable.

\Box

7
Definition of Multi-party PSI with Fair Compensation and Reward

In this section, we upgrade
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
to “multi-party PSI with Fair Compensation and Reward” (
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
), which (in addition to offering the features of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
) allows honest clients who contribute their set to receive a reward by a buyer who initiates the PSI computation and is interested in the result.

In
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
, there are (1) a set of clients
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
a subset of which is potentially active adversaries and may collude with each other, (2) a non-colluding dealer,
D
, potentially semi-honest, and (3) an auditor
Aud
potentially semi-honest, where all clients (except
{Aud}
) have input set. Furthermore, in
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
there are two “extractor” clients, say
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, where
(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\in\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\}
. These extractor clients volunteer to extract the (encoded) elements of the intersection and send them to a public bulletin board, i.e., a smart contract. In return, they will be paid.
We assume these two extractors act rationally only when they want to carry out the paid task of extracting the intersection and reporting it to the smart contract, so they can maximise their profit.
6
6

6

Thus, similar to any
A_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
, these extractors might be corrupted by an active adversary during the PSI computation.
For simplicity, we let client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}
be the buyer, i.e., the party which initiates the PSI computation and is interested in the result.

The formal definition of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
is built upon the definition of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FC}}
(presented in Section
4
); nevertheless, in
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
, we ensure that honest non-buyer clients receive a
reward
for participating in the protocol and revealing a portion of their inputs deduced from the result. We: (i) upgrade the predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}
to
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
to ensure that when honest clients receive the result, then an honest non-buyer client receives its deposit back plus a reward and a buyer client receives its deposit back minus the paid reward, and (ii) upgrade the predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}
to
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
to ensure when an adversary aborts in an unfair manner (i.e., aborts but learns the result) then an honest party receives its deposit back plus a predefined amount of compensation plus a reward. The other two predicates (i.e.,
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}
and
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}
) remain unchanged. Given the above changes, we denote the four predicates as
\bar{Q}:=(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
. Below, we present the formal definition of predicates
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
and
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
.

Definition 8
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
:
Delivery-with-Reward predicate)

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a stable ledger,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
be smart contract
sc
’s address,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Adr
be the address of an honest party,
\ddot{x}
be a fixed amount of coins, and
pram:=(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},\ddot{x})
. Let
R
be a reward function that takes as input the computation result:
res
, a party’s address:
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, a reward a party should receive for each unit of revealed information:
\ddot{l}
, and input size:
inSize
. Then
R
is defined as follows, if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
belongs to a non-buyer, then it returns the total amount that
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
should be rewarded and if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
belongs to a buyer client, then it returns the reward’s leftover that the buyer can collect, i.e.,
R(res,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},\ddot{l},inSize)\rightarrow\ddot{rew}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. Then, the delivery with reward predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}(pram,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},res,\ddot{l},inSize)
returns
1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
has sent
\ddot{x}
amount to
sc
and received at least
\ddot{x}+\ddot{rew}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
amount from it. Else, it returns
0
.

Definition 9
(
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
: UnFair-Abort-with-Reward predicate)

Let
pram:=(\mathcal{G},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},\ddot{x})
be the parameters defined above, and
Adr^{\prime}\subset Adr
be a set containing honest parties’ addresses,
m^{\prime}=|Adr^{\prime}|
, and
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Adr^{\prime}
. Let also
G
be a compensation function that takes as input three parameters
(\ddot{deps},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},m^{\prime})
, where
\ddot{deps}
is the amount of coins that all
m+1
parties deposit,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is an honest party’s address, and
m^{\prime}=|Adr^{\prime}|
; it returns the amount of compensation each honest party must receive, i.e.,
G(\ddot{deps},ard_{\scriptscriptstyle i},m^{\prime})\rightarrow\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
. Let
R
be the reward function defined above, i.e.,
R(res,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i},\ddot{l},inSize)\rightarrow\ddot{rew}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, and let
\hat{pram}:=(res,\ddot{l},inSize)
. Then, predicate
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}
is defined as
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}(pram,\hat{pram},G,R,\ddot{deps},m^{\prime},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\rightarrow(a,b)
, where
a=1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is an honest party’s address which has sent
\ddot{x}
amount to
sc
and received
\ddot{x}+\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+\ddot{rew}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
from it, and
b=1
if
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is an auditor’s address which received
\ddot{x}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
from
sc
. Otherwise,
a=b=0
.

Next, we present the formal definition of multi-party PSI with Fair Compensation and Reward,
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
.

Definition 10
(
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}
)

Let
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
be the multi-party PSI functionality defined in Section
4
. We say protocol
\Gamma
realises
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with
\bar{Q}
-fairness-and-reward in the presence of
m-3
static active-adversary clients
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s and
two
rational clients
A_{\scriptscriptstyle i}s
or a static passive dealer
D
or passive auditor
Aud
, if for every non-uniform probabilistic polynomial time adversary
\mathcal{A}
for the real model, there exists a non-uniform probabilistic polynomial-time adversary (or simulator)
\mathsf{Sim}
for the ideal model, such that for every
I\in\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m},D,Aud\}
, it holds that:

\{\mathsf{Ideal}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{W}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}},\bar{Q})}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Sim}(z),I}(S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1})\}_{\scriptscriptstyle S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1},z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c}}{{\equiv}}\{\mathsf{Real}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}(z),I}^{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma}(S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1})\}_{\scriptscriptstyle S_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,S_{\scriptscriptstyle m+1},z}

where
z
is an auxiliary input given to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{W}(f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}},\bar{Q})
is a functionality that wraps
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with predicates
\bar{Q}:=(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
.

8
Anesidora: A Concrete Construction of
\mathcal{PSI}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{FCR}}

8.1
Main Challenges to Overcome

Rewarding Clients Proportionate to the Intersection Cardinality.

In PSIs, the main private information about the clients which is revealed to a result recipient is the private set elements that the clients have in common. Thus, honest clients must receive a reward proportionate to the intersection cardinality, from a buyer. To receive the reward, the clients need to reach a consensus on the intersection cardinality. The naive way to do that is to let every client find the intersection and declare it to the smart contract. Under the assumption that the majority of clients are honest, then the smart contract can reward the honest result recipient (from the buyer’s deposit). Nevertheless, the honest majority assumption is strong in the context of multi-party PSI. Moreover, this approach requires all clients to extract the intersection, which would increase the overall costs. Some clients may not even be interested in or available to do so. This task could also be conducted by a single entity, such as the dealer; but this approach would introduce a single point of failure and all clients have to depend on this entity.
To address these challenges, we allow any two clients to become extractors. Each of them finds and sends to the contract the (encrypted) elements in the intersection. It is paid by the contract if the contract concludes that it is honest. This allows us to avoid (i) the honest majority assumption, (ii) requiring all clients to find the intersection, and (iii) relying on a single trusted/semi-honest party to complete the task.

Dealing with Extractors’ Collusion.

Using two extractors itself introduces another challenge; namely, they may collude with each other (and with the buyer) to provide a consistent but incorrect result, e.g., both may declare that only
s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
is in the intersection while
the actual intersection contains
100
set elements, including
s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
. This behaviour will not be detected by a verifier unless the verifier always conducts the delegated task itself too, which would defeat the purpose of delegation. To efficiently address this issue, we use the counter-collusion smart contracts (outlined in Section
3.4
) which creates distrust between the two extractors and incentivises them to act honestly.

8.2
Description of Anesidora (
\mathtt{ANE}
)

An Overview.

To construct
\mathtt{ANE}
, we mainly use
\mathtt{JUS}
, deterministic encryption, “double-layered” commitments, the hash-based padding technique (from Section
3.9
), and the counter-collusion smart contracts. At a high level,
\mathtt{ANE}
works as follows. First, all clients run step
1
of
\mathtt{JUS}
to agree on a set of parameters and
\mathtt{JUS}
’s smart contract. They deploy another smart contract, say
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. They also agree on a secret key,
mk^{\prime}
. Next, the buyer places a certain deposit into
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. This deposit will be distributed among honest clients as a reward.
The extractors and
D
deploy one of the counter-collusion smart contracts, i.e.,
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. These three parties deposit a certain amount on this contract. Each honest extractor will receive a portion of
D
’s deposit for carrying out its task honestly and each dishonest extractor will lose a portion of its deposit for acting maliciously.
Then, each client encrypts its set elements (under
mk^{\prime}
using deterministic encryption) and then represents the encrypted elements as a polynomial. The reason each client encrypts its set elements is to ensure that the privacy of the plaintext elements in the intersection will be preserved from the public.

Next, the extractors commit to the encryption of their set elements and publish the commitments.
All clients (including
D
) take the rest of the steps in
\mathtt{JUS}
using their input polynomials. This results in a blinded polynomial, whose correctness is checked by
\mathtt{JUS}
’s smart contact.

If
\mathtt{JUS}
’s smart contact approves the result’s correctness, then all parties receive the money that they deposited in
\mathtt{JUS}
’s contract. In this case, each extractor finds the set elements in the intersection. Each extractor proves to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
that the encryptions of the elements in the intersection are among the commitments that the extractor previously published.
If
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
accepts both extractors’ proofs, then it pays each client (except the buyer) a reward, where the reward is taken from the buyer’s deposit. The extractors receive their deposits back and are paid for carrying out the task honestly. Nevertheless, if
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
does not accept one of the extractors’ proofs (or one extractor betrays the other), then it invokes the auditor in the counter-collusion contracts to identify the misbehaving extractor. Then,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
pays each honest client (except the buyer) a reward, taken from the misbehaving extractor.
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
also refunds the buyer’s deposit.

If
\mathtt{JUS}
’s smart contact does not approve the result’s correctness and
{Aud}
identified misbehaving clients, then honest clients will receive (1) their deposit back from
\mathtt{JUS}
’s contract, and (2) compensation and reward, taken from misbehaving clients. Moreover, the buyer and extractors receive their deposit back from
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Figure
5
outlines the interaction between parties.

Figure 5:
Outline of the interactions between parties in Anesidora

Detailed Description of
\mathtt{ANE}
.

Next, we describe the protocol in more detail (Table
1
summarises the main notations used).

1.

All clients in
{CL}=\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m},D\}
together run step
1
of
\mathtt{JUS}
(in Section
6.2
) to deploy
\mathtt{JUS}
’s contract
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
and agree on a master key,
mk
.

2.

All clients in
{CL}
deploy a new smart contract,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. The address of
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
is given to all clients.

3.

The buyer, client
{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}
, before time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
deposits
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

4.

All clients after time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}>t_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
ensure that the buyer has deposited
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount on
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Otherwise, they abort.

5.

D
signs
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
with the extractors.
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
transfers
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{r}
amount (from the buyer deposit) to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
for each extractor. This is the maximum amount to be paid to an honest extractor for honestly declaring the elements of the intersection. Each extractor deposits
\ddot{d}^{\prime}=\ddot{d}+S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{f}
amount in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
at time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
. At time
t_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
all clients ensure that the extractors deposited enough coins; otherwise, they withdraw their deposit and abort.

6.

D
encrypts
mk
under the public key of the dispute resolver (in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
); let
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
be the resulting ciphertext. It also generates a commitment of
mk
as follows:
z^{\prime}=\mathtt{PRF}(mk,0),\ com_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}=\mathtt{Com}(mk,z^{\prime})
. It stores
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
and
{com}_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

7.

All clients in
{CL}
engage in
\mathtt{CT}
to agree on another key,
mk^{\prime}
.

8.

Each client in
{CL}
maps the elements of its set
S:\{s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,s_{\scriptscriptstyle c}\}
to random values by encrypting them as:
\forall i,1\leq i\leq c:e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\mathtt{PRP}(mk^{\prime},s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
.
Then, it encodes its encrypted set element as
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}||\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
.
After that, it constructs a hash table
\mathtt{HT}
and inserts the encoded elements into the table.
\forall i:\mathtt{H}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})={{{j}}}
, then
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\rightarrow\mathtt{HT}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{j}}}
. It pads every bin with random dummy elements to
d
elements (if needed). Then, for every bin, it builds a polynomial whose roots are the bin’s content:
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(I)}}}=\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle d}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-e^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
e^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is either
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, or a dummy value.

9.

Every extractor in
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\}
:

(a)

for each
j
-th bin, commits to the bin’s elements:
com_{\scriptscriptstyle{i,j}}=\mathtt{Com}(e^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is a fresh randomness used for the commitment and
e^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is either
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, or a dummy value of the bin.

(b)

constructs a Merkel tree on top of all committed values:
\mathtt{MT.genTree}(com_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1},...,com_{\scriptscriptstyle d,h})\rightarrow g
.

(c)

stores the Merkel tree’s root
g
on
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

10.

All clients in
{CL}
run steps
3
–
11
of
\mathtt{JUS}
, where each client now deposits (in the
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
)
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount where
\ddot{y}^{\prime}>S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}+{\ddot{ch}}
. Recall, at the end of step
11
of
\mathtt{JUS}
for each
j
-th bin (i) a random polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
has been registered in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, (ii) a polynomial
\bm{\phi}
(blinded by a random polynomial
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
) has been extracted by
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, and (iii)
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
has checked this polynomial’s correctness. If the latter check:

\bullet

passes (i.e.,
Flag=True
): all parties run step
12
of
\mathtt{JUS}
(with a minor difference, see Section
8.3
). In this case, each party receives
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount it deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. They proceed to step
11
below.

\bullet

fails (i.e.,
Flag=False
): all parties run step
13
of
\mathtt{JUS}
. In this case,
(as in
\mathtt{JUS}
)
{Aud}
is paid
\ddot{ch}
amount, and each honest party receives back its deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount. Also, from the misbehaving parties’ deposit
\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot\ddot{y}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
amount is sent to each honest client, to reward and compensate the client
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{l}
and
\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot\ddot{y}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}-S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{l}
amounts respectively, where
m^{\prime}
is the total number of misbehaving parties. Moreover,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
returns to the buyer its deposit (i.e.,
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount paid to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
), and returns to each extractor its deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount paid to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. Then, the protocol halts.

11.

Every extractor client:

(a)

finds the elements in the intersection. To do so, it first encodes each of its set elements to get
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, as explained in step
8
.
Then, it determines to which bin the encrypted value belongs, i.e.,
{{{j}}}=\mathtt{H}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
. Next, it evaluates the resulting polynomial (for that bin) at the encrypted element. It considers the element in the intersection if the evaluation is zero, i.e.,
\bm{\phi}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})-\bm{\zeta}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\cdot\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
. If the extractor is a traitor, by this point it should have signed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
with
{D}
and provided all the inputs (e.g., correct result) to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
.

(b)

proves that every element in the intersection is among the elements it has committed to. Specifically, for each element in the intersection, say
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, it sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
:

\bullet

commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
(generated in step
9a
, for
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
) and its opening
{\hat{x}}^{\prime}:=(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
.

\bullet

proof
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
that asserts
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is a leaf node of a Merkel tree with root
g
.

(c)

sends the opening of commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
, i.e., pair
\hat{x}:=(mk,z^{\prime})
, to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. This is done only once for all elements in the intersection.

12.

Contract
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
:

(a)

verifies the opening of the commitment for
mk
, i.e.,
\mathtt{Ver}(com_{\scriptscriptstyle mk},\hat{x})=1
. If the verification passes, it generates the index of the bin to which
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
belongs, i.e.,
{{{j}}}=\mathtt{H}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
. It uses
mk
to derive the pseudorandom polynomial
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
for
j
-th bin.

(b)

checks whether (i) the opening of commitment is valid, (ii) the Merkle tree proof is valid, and (iii) the encrypted element is the resulting polynomial’s root. Specifically, it ensures that the following relation holds:

\Bigg(\mathtt{Ver}(com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},\hat{x}^{\prime})=1\Bigg)\hskip 2.84526pt\wedge\hskip 2.84526pt\Bigg(\mathtt{MT.verify}(h_{\scriptscriptstyle i},g)=1\Bigg)\hskip 2.84526pt\wedge\hskip 2.84526pt\Bigg(\bm{\phi}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})-\bm{\zeta}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\cdot\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0\Bigg)

13.

The parties are paid as follows.

\bullet

if all proofs of both extractors are valid, both extractors provided identical elements of the intersections (for each bin), and there is no traitor, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
:

(a)

takes
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot m\cdot\ddot{l}
amount from the buyer’s deposit (in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) and distributes it among all clients, except the buyer.

(b)

calls
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
which returns the extractors’ deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount each) and pays each extractor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount, for doing their job correctly.

(c)

checks if
|{{{S}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|<S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}
. If the check passes, then it returns
(S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}-|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|)\cdot\ddot{v}
amount to the buyer.

\bullet

if both extractors failed to deliver any result, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
:

(a)

refunds the buyer, by sending
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount (deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) back to the buyer.

(b)

retrieves each extractor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}
amount) from the
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
and distributes it among the rest of the clients (except the buyer and extractors).

\bullet

Otherwise (e.g., if some proofs are invalid, if an extractor’s result is inconsistent with the other extractor’s result, or there is a traitor),
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
invokes (steps 8.c and 9 of)
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
and its auditor to identify the misbehaving extractor, with the help of
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
after decrypting it. Then,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
asks
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
to pay the auditor the total amount of
\ddot{ch}
taken from the deposit of the extractor(s) who provided incorrect result to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Moreover,

(a)

if
both extractors cheated
:

i.

if there
is no traitor
, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
refunds the buyer, by sending
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount (deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) back to the buyer. It also distributes
2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}
amount (taken from the extractors’ deposit in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
) among the rest of clients (except the buyer and extractors).

ii.

if there
is a traitor
, then:

A.

if the traitor delivered a
correct result
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
retrieves
\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{d}
amount from the other dishonest extractor’s deposit (in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
) and distributes it among the rest of the clients (except the buyer and dishonest extractor). Also, it asks
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
to send
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}+\ddot{d}^{\prime}+\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount to the traitor (via
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
).
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
refunds the traitor’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount. It refunds the buyer, by sending
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}-|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount (deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) back to it.

B.

if the traitor delivered an
incorrect result
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
pays the buyer and rest of clients in the same way it does in step
13(a)i
.

\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
refunds the traitor, i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount.

(b)

if
one of the extractors cheated
:

i.

if there
is no traitor
, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
calls
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
that (a) returns the honest extractor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount), (b) pays this extractor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount, for doing its job honestly, and (c) pays this extractor
\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount taken from the dishonest extractor’s deposit.

\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
pays the buyer and the rest of the clients in the same way it does in step
13iiA
.

ii.

if there
is a traitor

A.

if the traitor delivered a
correct result
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
(but it cheated in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
), then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
calls
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
that (a) returns the other honest extractor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount), (b) pays the honest extractor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount taken from the buyer’s deposit, for doing its job honestly, (c) pays the honest extractor
\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount taken from the traitor’s deposit,
(d)
pays to the traitor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount taken from the buyer’s deposit (via the
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
), and (e) refunds the traitor
\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{d}
amount taken from its own deposit.
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
refunds the traitor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount).
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
takes
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot m\cdot\ddot{l}
amount from the buyer’s deposit (in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) and distributes it among all clients, except the buyer. If
|{{{S}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|<S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}
, then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
returns
(S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}-|{{{S}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|)\cdot\ddot{v}
amount (deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
) back to the buyer.

B.

if the traitor delivered an
incorrect result
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
(and it cheated in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
), then
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
pays the honest extractor in the same way it does in step
13iiA
.

\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
refunds the traitor’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount.
Also,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
pays the buyer and the rest of the clients in the same way it does in step
13iiA
.

Theorem 8.1

If
\mathtt{PRP}
,
\mathtt{PRF}
, the commitment scheme, smart contracts, the Merkle tree scheme,
\mathtt{JUS}
and the counter-collusion contracts are secure and the public key encryption is semantically secure, then
\mathtt{ANE}
realises
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{PSI}}
with
\bar{Q}
-fairness-and-reward (w.r.t. Definition
10
) in the presence of
m-3
static active-adversary clients
A_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
s and
two
rational clients
A_{\scriptscriptstyle i}s
or a static passive dealer
D
or passive auditor
Aud
, or passive public which sees the intersection cardinality.

Before we prove Theorem
8.1
in Section
8.4
, we present several remarks on the
\mathtt{ANE}
.

8.3
Further Discussion on Anesidora

There is a simpler but costlier approach to finding the intersection without involving the extractors; that is the smart contract finds the (encoded) elements of the intersection and distributes the parties’ deposit according to the number of elements it finds. This approach is simpler, as we do not need the involvement of (i) the extractors and (ii) the three counter collusion contracts. Nevertheless, it is costlier, because the contract itself needs to factorise the unblinded resulting polynomial and find the roots, which would cost it
O(d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
for each bin, where
d
is the size of each bin. Our proposed approach however moves such a computation off-chain, leading to a lower monetary computation cost.

The reason each client uses the hash-based padding to encode each encrypted element
e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
as
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}||\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
is to allow the auditor in the counter collusion contracts to find the error-free intersection, without having to access to one of the original (encrypted) sets.

Compared to
\mathtt{JUS}
, there is a minor difference in finding the result in
\mathtt{ANE}
. Specifically, because in
\mathtt{ANE}
each set element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is encoded as (i)
e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\mathtt{PRP}(mk^{\prime},s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
and then (ii)
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}||\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
by a client, then when the client wants to find the intersection it needs to first regenerate
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
as above and then treat it as a set element to check if
\bm{\phi}^{\prime}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
, in step
12(b)iii
of
\mathtt{JUS}
.

In
\mathtt{ANE}
, each extractor uses double-layered commitments (i.e., it first commits to the encryption of each element and then constructs a Merkle tree on top of all commitments) for efficiency and privacy purposes. Constructing a Merkle tree on top of the commitments allows the extractor to store only a single value in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
would impose a much lower storage cost compared to the case where it would store all commitments in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Also, committing to the elements’ encryption allows it to hide from other clients the encryption of those elements that are not in the intersection. Recall that encrypting each element is not sufficient to protect one client’s elements from the rest of the clients, as they all know the decryption key.

To increase their reward, malicious clients may be tempted to insert “garbage” elements into their sets with the hope that those garbage elements appear in the result and accordingly they receive a higher reward. However, they would not succeed as long as there exists a semi-honest client (e.g., dealer
D
) which uses actual set elements. In this case, by the set intersection definition, those garbage elements will not appear in the intersection.

In
\mathtt{ANE}
, for the sake of simplicity, we let each party receive a fixed reward, i.e.,
\ddot{l}
, for every element it contributes to the intersection. However, it is possible to make the process more flexible/generic. For instance, we could define a Reward Function
RF
that takes
\ddot{l}
, an (encoded) set element
e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in the intersection, its distribution/value
val_{\scriptscriptstyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
, and output a reward
rew_{\scriptscriptstyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
that each party should receive for contributing that element to the intersection, i.e.,
RF(\ddot{l},e_{\scriptscriptstyle i},val_{\scriptscriptstyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}})\rightarrow rew_{\scriptscriptstyle e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}
.

8.4
Proof of
\mathtt{ANE}

In this section, we prove Theorem
8.1
, i.e., the security of
\mathtt{ANE}
.

Proof

We prove the theorem by considering the case where each party is corrupt, at a time.

Case 1: Corrupt extractors
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\}
and
m-3
clients in
\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
. Let set
G
include extractors
\{A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\}
and a set of at most
m-3
corrupt clients in
\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
. Let set
\hat{G}
be a set of honest clients in
\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
. Also, let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
be the simulator. We let the simulator interact with (i) active adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that may corrupt
m-3
clients in
\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
, and (ii) two rational adversaries
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}:=(\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
that corrupt extractors
(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
component-wise.
In the simulation, before the point where the extractors are invoked to provide proofs and results, the simulator directly deals with active adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. However, when the extractors are involved (to generate proofs and extract the result) we require the simulator to interact with each rational adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. We allow these two subroutine adversaries
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime})
to internally interact with each other. Now, we explain how
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
, which receives the input sets of honest dealer
D
and honest client(s) in
\hat{G}
, works.

1.

constructs and deploys two smart contracts (for
\mathtt{JUS}
and
\mathtt{ANE}
). It sends the contracts’ addresses to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. It also simulates
\mathtt{CT}
and receives the output value,
{mk}
, from its functionality,
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
.

2.

deposits
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
if buyer
A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}
is honest, i.e.,
A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}\in\hat{G}
. Otherwise,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}}
checks if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has deposited
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. If the check fails, it instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited and sends message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP (and accordingly to all parties); it outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs and then halts.

3.

constructs and deploys a (Prisoner’s) contract and transfers
\ddot{w}=S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{r}
amount for each extractor.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
ensures that each extractor deposited
\ddot{d}^{\prime}=\ddot{d}+S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{f}
coins in this contract; otherwise, it instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited and sends message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP; it outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs and then halts.

4.

encrypts
mk
under the public key of the dispute resolver; let
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
be the resulting ciphertext. It also generates a commitment of
{mk}
as follows:
com_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}=\mathtt{Com}({mk},\mathtt{PRF}({mk},0))
. It stores
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
and
{com}_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

5.

simulates
\mathtt{CT}
again and receives the output value
{mk}^{\prime}
from
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
.

6.

receives from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
a Merkle tree’s root
g^{\prime}
for each extractor.

7.

simulates the steps of
3
–
11
in
\mathtt{JUS}
