. For completeness, we include the steps that the simulator takes in this proof. Specifically,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{{}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}}
:

(a)

simulates
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
for each bin and receives the output value
(k,g,q)
from
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}}
.

(b)

deposits in the contract the amount of
\ddot{y}^{\prime}=S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}+\ddot{ch}
for client
D
and each honest client in
\hat{G}
. It sends to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
the amount deposited in the contract.

(c)

checks if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has deposited
\ddot{y}^{\prime}\cdot|G|
amount (in addition to
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount deposited in step
3
above). If the check fails, it instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited and sends message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP (and accordingly to all parties); it outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs and then halts.

(d)

picks a random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
of degree
1
, for each bin.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
, for each client
{C}\in\{{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},...,{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}\}
allocates to each bin two degree
d
random polynomials: (
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}},{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
), and two degree
3d+1
random polynomials: (
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
,
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
). Also,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
for each honest client
C^{\prime}\in\hat{G}
, for each bin, picks two degree
d
random polynomials: (
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}
,
{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}
).

(e)

simulates
\mathtt{VOPR}
using inputs
{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
and
{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
for each bin. It receives the inputs of clients
C^{\prime\prime}\in G
, i.e.,
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, from its functionality
f^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}
, for each bin.

(f)

extracts the roots of polynomial
{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
for each bin and appends those roots that are in the sets universe to a new set
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime\prime})}
.

(g)

simulates again
\mathtt{VOPR}
using inputs
{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
and
{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}
, for each bin.

(h)

sends to TTP the input sets of all parties; specifically, (i) client
D
’s input set:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
, (ii) honest clients’ input sets:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime})}
for all
C^{\prime}
in
\hat{G}
, and (iii)
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
’s input sets:
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(C^{\prime\prime})}
, for all
C^{\prime\prime}
in
G
. For each bin, it receives the intersection set,
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
, from TTP.

(i)

represents the intersection set for each bin as a polynomial,
{\bm{\pi}}
, as follows. First, it encrypts each element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
of
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
as
e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\mathtt{PRP}({mk}^{\prime},s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
. Second, it encodes each encrypted element as
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle i}||\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
. Third, it constructs
{\bm{\pi}}
as
{\bm{\pi}}=\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\cdot\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle d-|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=1}(x-u_{\scriptscriptstyle j})
, where
u_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
is a dummy value.

(j)

constructs polynomials
{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}},{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}\cdot{\bm{\rho}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime},D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime})}}}
, and
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}={\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+{\bm{\theta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
, for each bin and each honest client
C^{\prime}\in\hat{G}
, such that
{\bm{\tau}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle 3d+2}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=0}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and each value
z_{\scriptscriptstyle i,c}
is derived from key
k
generated in step
7a
. It sends to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime})}}}
for each bin and each honest client
C^{\prime}\in\hat{G}
.

(k)

receives
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, for each bin and each corrupt client
C^{\prime\prime}\in G
. It checks whether the output for every
C^{\prime\prime}
has been provided. Otherwise, it halts.

(l)

if there is any abort within steps
7e
–
7k
, then it sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to refund the coins that every party deposited. It outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs and then halts.

(m)

constructs polynomial
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}={\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\omega}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}\cdot{\bm{\pi}}-\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{m}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{C}=}\scriptscriptstyle{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}}({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C)}}})+{\bm{\zeta}}\cdot{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
for each bin on behalf of client
D
, where
{\bm{\omega}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
is a fresh random polynomial of degree
d
and
{\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}}
is a pseudorandom polynomial derived from
{mk}
.

(n)

sends to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
and
{\bm{\zeta}}
for each bin.

(o)

computes polynomial
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}
as
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in G}{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}-\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle\forall C^{\prime\prime}\in G}({\bm{\gamma}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}}+{\bm{\delta}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D,C^{\prime\prime})}}})
, for every bin. Next, it checks if
{\bm{\zeta}}
divides
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}
, for every bin. If the check passes, it sets
Flag=True
. Otherwise, it sets
Flag=False
.

(p)

if
Flag=True
, then instructs the ledger to send back each party’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount. It sends a message
deliver
to TTP. It proceeds to step
8
below.

if

:

i.

receives

keys of the

from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern , for every bin.

or

. Then, it sends message

to TTP.

.

encrypts each element

as

. Then, it encodes each encrypted element as

. Let set

include all encoded encrypted elements in the intersection.

sings a
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
with

, if

.

matches the opening

and the opening corresponds to a unique element in

.

. Specifically, given the proof and root

is a leaf node of a Merkle tree with a root node

. It also checks whether the opening

matches

.

if all the checks in steps

–

pass, then instructs the ledger (i) to take

amount from the buyer’s deposit and distributes it among all clients, except the buyer, (ii) to return the extractors deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount each) and pay each extractor

amount, and (iii) to return

if neither extractor sends the extractor’s set intersection
(E^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})},E^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})})
in step
8
, then instructs the ledger (i) to refund the buyer, by sending

amount back to the buyer and (ii) to

amount) from

and distribute it among the rest of the clients (except the buyer and extractors).

16.

if the checks in step

fail or in step

both
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
send the extractors’ set intersection but they are inconsistent with each other, then it tags the extractor whose proof or set intersection was invalid as a misbehaving extractor.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
instructs the ledger to pay the auditor (of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
) the total amount of
\ddot{ch}
coins taken from the misbehaving extractor(s) deposit. Furthermore,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
takes the following steps.

(a)

if both extractors cheated and there is no traitor, then instructs the ledger (i) to refund the buyer
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
amount, and (ii) to take
2\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}
amount from the misbehaving extractors’ deposit and distribute it to the rest of the clients except the buyer and extractors.

(b)

if both extractors cheated, there is a traitor, and the traitor delivered a correct result (in step
10
), then instructs the ledger (i) to take
\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{d}
amount from the other misbehaving extractor’s deposit and distribute it among the rest of the clients (except the buyer and dishonest extractor), (ii) to distribute
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}+\ddot{d}^{\prime}+\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount to the traitor, (iii) to refund the traitor
\ddot{ch}
amount, and (iv) to refund the buyer
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}-|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount.

(c)

if both extractors cheated, there is a traitor, and the traitor delivered an incorrect result (in step
10
), then instructs the ledger to distribute coins the same way it does in step
16a
.

(d)

if one of the extractors cheated and there is no traitor, then instructs the ledger (i) to return the honest extractor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount), (ii) to pay the honest extractor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount, (iii) to pay this extractor
\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount taken from the dishonest extractor’s deposit, and (iv) to pay the buyer and the rest of the clients the same way it does in step
16b
.

(e)

if one of the extractors cheated, there is a traitor, and the traitor delivered a correct result (in step
10
), then instructs the ledger (i) to return the other honest extractor’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount), (ii) to pay the honest extractor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount, taken from the buyer’s deposit, (iii) to pay the honest extractor
\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount, taken from the traitor’s deposit, (iv) to pay to the traitor
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{r}
amount, taken from the buyer’s deposit, (v) to refund the traitor
\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{d}
amount, (vi) to refund the traitor
\ddot{ch}
amount, (vii) to take
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot m\cdot\ddot{l}
amount from the buyer’s deposit and distribute it among all clients, except the buyer, and (viii) to return
(S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}-|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|)\cdot\ddot{v}
amount back to the buyer.

(f)

if one of the extractors cheated, there is a traitor, and the traitor delivered an incorrect result (in step
10
), then instructs the ledger (i) to pay the honest extractor the same way it does in step
13iiA
, (ii) to refund the traitor
\ddot{ch}
amount, and (iii) to pay the buyer and the rest of the clients in the same way it does in step
16b
.

(g)

outputs whatever
\mathcal{A}
outputs and then halts.

Next, we show that the real and ideal models are computationally indistinguishable. We first focus on the adversary’s output. The addresses of the smart contracts have identical distribution in both models. In the real and ideal models, the adversary sees the transcripts of ideal calls to
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
as well as the functionality outputs (
mk,mk^{\prime}
). Due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
(as we are in the
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
-hybrid world), the transcripts of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
in both models have identical distribution, so have the random outputs of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}
, i.e., (
mk,mk^{\prime}
). Also, the deposit amounts
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
and
\ddot{w}
have identical distributions in both models. Due to the semantical security of the public key encryption, the ciphertext
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
in the real model is computationally indistinguishable from the ciphertext
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
in the ideal model. Due to the hiding property of the commitment scheme, commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
in the real model is computationally indistinguishable from commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
in the ideal model.
Moreover, due to the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
, all transcripts and outputs produced in the ideal model, in step
7
above, have identical distribution to the corresponding transcripts and outputs produced in
\mathtt{JUS}
in the real model.
The address of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
has the same distribution in both models. The amounts each party receives in the real and ideal models are the same, except when both extractors produce an identical and incorrect result (i.e., intersection) in the real model, as we will shortly discuss, this would not occur under the assumption that the extractors are rational and due to the security of the counter collusion smart contracts.

Now, we show that an honest party aborts with the same probability in the real and ideal models. As before, for the sake of completeness, we include the
\mathtt{JUS}
in the following discussion as well. Due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
, an honest party, during
\mathtt{CT}
invocation, aborts with the same probability in both models; in this case, the adversary learns nothing about the parties’ input set and the sets’ intersection as the parties have not sent out any encoded input set yet. In both models, an honest party can read the smart contract and check if sufficient amounts of coins have been deposited. Thus, it would halt with the same probability in both models. If the parties halt because of insufficient amounts of deposit, no one could learn about (i) the parties’ input set and (ii) the sets’ intersection because the inputs (representation) have not been dispatched at this point. Due to the security of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, an honest party during
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
execution aborts with the same probability in both models. In this case, an aborting adversary also learns nothing about the parties’ input set and the sets’ intersection.

Due to the security of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, honest parties abort with the same probability in both models. In the case where a party aborts during the execution of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, the adversary would learn nothing (i) about its counter party’s input set, and (ii) about the rest of the honest parties’ input sets and the intersection as the other parties’ input sets remain blinded by random blinding factors known only to client
D
. In the real model, client
D
can check if all parties provided their encoded inputs via reading the state of the smart contract. The simulator can perform the same check to ensure
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has provided the encoded inputs of all corrupt parties. So, in both models, an honest party with the same probability detects if not all encoded inputs have been provided. In this case, if an adversary aborts and does not provide its encoded inputs (i.e., polynomials
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
), then it learns nothing about the honest parties’ input sets and the intersection, for the same reason explained above.

In the real model, the contract sums every client
C
’s polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
with each other and with client
D
’s polynomial
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(D)}}}
, that ultimately removes the blinding factors that
D
initially inserted (during the
\mathtt{VOPR}
execution), and then checks if the result is divisible by
\bm{\zeta}
. Due to (a) Theorem
5.7
, (b) the fact that the smart contract is given the random polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
in plaintext, (c) no party (except honest
D
) knew polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
before they send their input to the contract, and (d) the security of the contract (i.e., the adversary cannot influence the correctness of the smart contract’s verifications), the contract can detect if a set of outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
were tampered with, with a probability at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
. In the ideal model,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
(in step
7o
) can remove the blinding factors and it knows the random polynomial
{\bm{\zeta}}
. So,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can detect when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
tampers with a set of the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
(sent to
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
) with a probability at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
, due to Theorem
5.7
. Therefore, the smart contract in the real model and the simulator in the ideal model would abort with a similar probability.

Due to the security of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
, the probability that in the real model an invalid
k_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle k\hfil$\crcr}}}
is appended to
L
is similar to the probability that
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
detects an invalid
k^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle k^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}
in the ideal model. In the real model, when
Flag=False
, the smart contract can identify each ill-structured output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
(i.e.,
\bm{\nu}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
) with a probability of at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
by checking whether
\bm{\zeta}
divides
\bm{\iota}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
, due to (a) Theorem
5.6
(i.e., unforgeable polynomial), (b) the fact that the smart contract is given
\bm{\zeta}
in plaintext, (c) no party (except honest client
D
) knew anything about
\bm{\zeta}
before they send their input to the contract, and (d) the security of the contract.
In the ideal model, when
Flag=False
, given each
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can remove its blinding factors from
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
which results in
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
and then can check if
{\bm{\zeta}}
divides
{\bm{\phi}^{\prime}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
, in step
7(q)vi
.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can detect an ill-structured
{\bm{\nu}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C^{\prime\prime})}}}
with a probability of at least
1-\epsilon(\lambda)
, due to Theorem
5.6
, the fact that the simulator is given
{\bm{\zeta}}
in plaintext, and the adversary is not given any knowledge about
{\bm{\zeta}}
before it sends to the simulator the outputs of
\mathtt{VOPR}
. Therefore, the smart contract in the real model and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
in the ideal model can detect an ill-structured input of an adversary with the same probability.
The smart contract in the real model and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
in the ideal model can detect and abort with the same probability if the adversary provides an invalid opening to each commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
and
com_{\scriptscriptstyle{mk}}
, due to the binding property of the commitment scheme. Also, the smart contract in the real model and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
in the ideal model, can abort with the same probability if a Merkle tree proof is invalid, due to the security of the Merkle tree, i.e., due to the collision resistance of Merkle tree’s hash function.

Note that in the ideal model,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can detect and abort with a probability of
1
, if
\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle I}
does not send to the simulator all encoded encrypted elements of the intersection, i.e., when
E^{\scriptscriptstyle(I)}\neq S^{\prime}
. Because the simulator already knows all elements in the intersection (and the encryption key). Thus, it can detect with a probability of
1
if both the intersection sets that the extractors provide are identical but incorrect.
In the real world, if the extractors collude with each other and provide identical but incorrect intersections, then an honest client (or the smart contract) cannot detect it. Thus, the adversary can distinguish the two models, based on the probability of aborting. However, under the assumption that the smart contracts (of Dong
et al.

[
18
]
) are secure (i.e., are counter-collusion), and the extractors are rational, such an event (i.e., providing identical but incorrect result without one extractor betraying the other) would not occur in either model, as the real model and
(\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\mathcal{A}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
rational adversaries follow the strategy that leads to a higher payoff. Specifically, as shown in
[
18
]
, providing incorrect but identical results is not the preferred strategy of the extractors; instead, the betrayal of one extractor by the other is the most profitable strategy in the case of (enforceable) collusion between the two extractors. This also implies that the amounts that the extractors would receive in both models are identical.

Now, we analyse the output of the predicates
\bar{Q}:=(Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}},Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}})
in the real and ideal models. In the real model, all clients proceed to prepare their input set only if the predefined amount of coins have been deposited by the parties; otherwise (if in steps
2
,
4
,
5
of
\mathtt{ANE}
and step
4
of
\mathtt{JUS}
there is not enough deposit), they will be refunded and the protocol halts. In the ideal model, the simulator proceeds to prepare its inputs only if enough deposit has been placed in the contract. Otherwise, it would send message
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
to TTP, during steps
2
–
7c
. Thus, in both models, the parties proceed to prepare their inputs only if
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Init}}(.)\rightarrow 1
.
In the real model, if there is an abort after the parties ensure there is enough deposit and before client
D
provides its encoded input to the contract, then all parties can retrieve their deposit in full. In this case, the aborting adversary cannot learn anything about honest parties’ input sets, as the parties’ input sets have been blinded by random blinding polynomials known only to client
D
. In the ideal model, if there is any abort during steps
7e
–
7k
, then the simulator sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to refund every party’s deposit. In the case of an abort, within the above two steps, the auditor is not involved, and paid. Therefore, in both models, in the case of an abort within the above steps, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{F-A}}(.)\rightarrow 1
.

In the real model, if
Flag=True
, then all parties can locally extract the intersection, regardless of the extractors’ behaviour. In this case, each honest party receives
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount that it initially deposited in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
. Moreover, each honest party receives
at least

|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{l}
amount as a reward, for contributing to the result. In this case, the honest buyer always collects the leftover of its deposit. Specifically, if both extractors act honestly, and the intersection cardinality is smaller than
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}|
, then the buyer collects its deposit leftover, after paying all honest parties. If any extractor misbehaves, then the honest buyer fully recovers its deposit (and the misbehaving extractor pays the rest). Even in the case that an extractor misbehaves and then becomes a traitor to correct its past misbehaviour, the buyer collects its deposit leftover if the intersection cardinality is smaller than
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}|
. In the ideal model, when
Flag=True
, then
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
can extract the intersection by summing the output of
\mathtt{VOPR}
provided by all parties and removing the blinding polynomials. In this case, it sends back each party’s deposit placed in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, i.e.,
\ddot{y}^{\prime}
amount. Also, in this case, each honest party receives at least
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\ddot{l}
amount as a reward and the honest buyer always collects the leftover of its deposit. Thus, in both models in the case of
Flag=True
, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{Del}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}(.)\rightarrow 1
.

In the real model, when
Flag=False
, only the adversary can learn the result. In this case, the contract sends (i)
\ddot{ch}
amount to
{Aud}
, and (ii)
\frac{m^{\prime}\cdot\ddot{y}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}}{m-m^{\prime}}
amount, as compensation and reward, to each honest party, in addition to each party’s initial deposit. In the ideal model, when
Flag=False
,
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle A}
sends
abort_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
to TTP and instructs the ledger to distribute the same amount the contract distributes among the auditor (e.g., with address
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
) and every honest party (e.g., with address
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
) in the real model. Thus, in both models when
Flag=False
, we would have
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}(.,.,.,.,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\rightarrow(a=1,.)
and
Q^{\scriptscriptstyle\text{UF-A}}_{\scriptscriptstyle\text{R}}(.,.,.,.,adr_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\rightarrow(.,b=1)
.

We conclude that the distribution of the joint outputs of the honest client
C\in\hat{G}
, client
D
,
{Aud}
, and the adversary in the real and ideal models are computationally indistinguishable.

Case 2: Corrupt dealer
D
. In the real execution, the dealer’s view is defined as follows:

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}\Big(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},...,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(m)})\Big)=

\{S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},S_{\scalebox{.52}{{min}}}\cdot\ddot{v},2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime},r_{\scriptscriptstyle D},\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D},\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D},(com_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})...,(com_{\scriptscriptstyle sz,j^{\prime}},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle sz},q_{\scriptscriptstyle{sz}},h_{\scriptscriptstyle sz}),g,\hat{x}:=({mk},z^{\prime}),S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}\}

where
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{VOPR}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
refer to
D
’s real-model view during the execution of
\mathtt{CT}
and
\mathtt{VOPR}
respectively. Also,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
is the outcome of internal random coins of
D
,
adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
is the address of contract,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
,
(j,...,j^{\prime})\in\{1,...,h\}
,
z^{\prime}=\mathtt{PRF}(\bar{mk},0)
,
sz=|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|
, and
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is a Merkle tree proof asserting that
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is a leaf node of a Merkle tree with root node
g
. The simulator
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
, which receives all parties’ input sets, works as follows.

1.

generates an empty view. It appends to the view, the input set
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
. It constructs and deploys a smart contract. It appends the contract’s address,
{adr}_{\scriptscriptstyle sc}
, to the view.

2.

appends to the view integer
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
, and
2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime}
. Also, it appends uniformly random coins
r^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
to the view.

3.

extracts the simulation of
\mathtt{JUS}
from
\mathtt{JUS}
’s simulator for client
D
. Let
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
be the simulation, that also includes a random key
mk
. It appends
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
to the view.

4.

extracts the simulation of
\mathtt{CT}
from
\mathtt{CT}
’s simulator, yielding the simulation
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
that includes its output
mk^{\prime}
. It appends
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
to the view.

5.

encrypts each element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
in the intersection set
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
as
e_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{PRP}(mk^{\prime},s_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
and then encodes the result as
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=e_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}||\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
. It commits to each encoded value as
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}=\mathtt{Com}(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
, where
j
is the index of the bin to which
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
belongs and
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is a random value.

6.

It constructs
(com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1},...,com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,h})
where each
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is a value picked uniformly at random from the commitment scheme output range. For every
j
-th bin, it sets each
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}
to unique
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
if value
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
for
j
-th bin has been generated in step
5
. Otherwise, the original values of
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}
remains unchanged.

7.

constructs a Merkle tree on top of the values
(com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,1},...,com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle d,h})
generated in step
6
. Let
g
be the resulting tree’s root.

8.

for each element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
in the intersection, it constructs
(com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},h_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
, where
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}
is the commitment of
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
,
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}\in com
,
(\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}

q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
is the commitment’s opening generated in step
5
, and
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
is a Merkle tree proof asserting that
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}
is a leaf of a Merkle tree with root
g
. It appends all
(com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j},h_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j})
along with
g
to the view.

9.

generates a commitment to
mk
as
com_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}=\mathtt{Com}(mk,z^{\prime})
where
z^{\prime}=\mathtt{PRF}(mk,0)
. It appends
(mk,z^{\prime})
along with
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
to the view.

Now, we will discuss why the two views are computationally indistinguishable.
D
’s input
S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)}
is
identical in both models, so they have identical distributions. The contract’s address has the same distribution in both models. The same holds for the integers
S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v}
and
2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime}
. Also, because the real-model semi-honest adversary samples its randomness according to the protocol’s description, the random coins in both models (i.e.,
r_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
r^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
) have identical distribution. Due to the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
,
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
have identical distribution, so do
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
and
\mathsf{Sim}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{CT}}_{\scriptscriptstyle D}
due to the security of
\mathtt{CT}
. The intersection elements in both models have identical distributions and the encryption scheme is schematically secure. Therefore, each intersection element’s representation (i.e.,
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in the real model and
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
in the deal model) are computationally indistinguishable. Each
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
in the real model and
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
in the ideal model have identical distributions as they have been picked uniformly at random. Each commitment
com_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
in the real model is computationally indistinguishable from commitment
com^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i^{\prime},j}
in the ideal model.

In the real model, each Merkle tree proof
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
contains two leaf nodes (along with intermediary nodes that are the hash values of a subset of leaf nodes) that are themselves the commitment values. Also, for each
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, only one of the leaf node’s openings (that contains an element in the intersection) is seen by
D
. The same holds in the ideal model, with the difference that for each Merkle tree proof
h_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
the leaf node whose opening is not provided is a random value, instead of an actual commitment. However, due to the hiding property of the commitment scheme, in the real and ideal models, these two leaf nodes (whose openings are not provided) and accordingly the two proofs are computationally indistinguishable. In both models, values
mk
and
z^{\prime}
are random values, so they have identical distributions. Furthermore, the intersection
S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}
is identical in both models. Thus, we conclude that the two views are computationally indistinguishable.

Case 3: Corrupt auditor
. In this case, the real-model view of the auditor is defined as

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle Aud}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}\Big(\bot,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)},...,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(m)})\Big)=

\{\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Aud},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v},2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime},(com_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})...,(com_{\scriptscriptstyle sz,j^{\prime}},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle sz},q_{\scriptscriptstyle{sz}},h_{\scriptscriptstyle sz}),g,\hat{x}:=({mk},z^{\prime})\}

Due to the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
,
{Aud}
’s view
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Aud}
during the execution of
\mathtt{JUS}
can be easily simulated. As we have shown in Case 2, for the remaining transcript, its real-model view can be simulated too. However, there is a difference between Case 3 and Case 2; namely, in the former case,
{Aud}
does not have the
\mathtt{PRP}
’s key
mk^{\prime}
used to encrypt each set element. However, due to the security of
\mathtt{PRP}
, it cannot distinguish each encrypted encoded element from a uniformly random element and cannot distinguish
\mathtt{PRP}(mk^{\prime},.)
from a uniform permutation. Therefore, each value
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
in the real model has identical distribution to each value
\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle i,j}
(as defined in Case 2) in the ideal model, as both are the outputs of
\mathtt{PRP}
.

Case 4: Corrupt public
. In the real model, the view of the public (i.e., non-participants of the protocol) is defined as below:

\mathsf{View}_{\scriptscriptstyle Pub}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{ANE}}\Big(\bot,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(D)},(S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{1})},...,S^{\scriptscriptstyle(A_{m})})\Big)=

\{\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Pub},adr_{\scriptscriptstyle sc},S_{\scriptscriptstyle min}\cdot\ddot{v},2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime},(com_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,j},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})...,(com_{\scriptscriptstyle sz,j^{\prime}},\bar{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle sz},q_{\scriptscriptstyle{sz}},h_{\scriptscriptstyle sz}),g,\hat{x}:=({mk},z^{\prime})\}

Due to the security of
\mathtt{JUS}
, the public’s view
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Pub}
during
\mathtt{JUS}
’s execution can be simulated in the same way which is done in Case 4, in Section
6.3
. The rest of the public’s view overlaps with
{Aud}
’s view in Case 3, excluding
\mathsf{View}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{JUS}}_{\scriptscriptstyle Aud}
. Therefore, we can use the argument provided in Case 3 to show that the rest of the public’s view can be simulated.
We conclude that the public’s real and ideal views are computationally indistinguishable.

\Box

9
Evaluation

In this section, we analyse the asymptotic costs of
\mathtt{ANE}
. We also compare its costs and features with the fastest two and multiple parties PSIs in
[
2
,
35
,
39
,
42
]
) and with the fair PSIs in
[
15
,
17
]
. Tables
2
and
3
summarise the result of the cost analysis and the comparison respectively.

Asymptotic costs of different parties in
\mathtt{ANE}
. In the table,
h
is the total number of bins,
d
is a bin’s capacity (i.e.,
d=100
),
m
is the total number of clients (excluding
D
),
|S|
is a set cardinality, and
\bar{\xi}
is
\mathtt{OLE}
’s security parameter.

Party

Computation Cost

Communication Cost

Client
