9.1
Computation Cost

Client’s and Dealer’s Costs.

In step
1
, the cost of each client (including dealer
D
) is
O(m)
and mainly involves an invocation of
\mathtt{CT}
.
In steps
2
–
5
, the clients’ cost is negligible as it involves deploying smart contracts and reading from the deployed contracts.
In step
7
, the clients’ cost is
O(m)
, as they need to invoke an instance of
\mathtt{CT}
.
In step
8
, each client invokes
\mathtt{PRP}
and
\mathtt{H}
linear with its set’s cardinality. In the same step, it also constructs
h
polynomials, where the construction of each polynomial involves
d
modular multiplications and additions. Thus, its complexity in this step is
O(h\cdot d)
. It has been shown in
[
2
]
that
O(h\cdot d)=O(|S|)
and
d=100
for all set sizes.

In step
10
, each client
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,A_{\scriptscriptstyle m}
(excluding
D
): (i) invokes an instance of
\mathtt{ZSPA\text{-}A}
which involves
O(h\cdot m)
invocation of
\mathtt{CT}
,
3h\cdot m(d+1)
invocation of
\mathtt{PRF}
,
3h\cdot m(d+1)
addition, and
O(h\cdot m\cdot d)
invocation of
\mathtt{H}
(in step
3
of subroutine
\mathtt{JUS}
), (ii) invokes
2h
instances of
\mathtt{VOPR}
, where each
\mathtt{VOPR}
invocation involves
2d(1+d)
invocations of
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
, multiplications, and additions (in steps
6
and
7
of
\mathtt{JUS}
), and (iii) performs
h(3d+2)
modular addition (in step
8
of
\mathtt{JUS}
).
Also, if
Flag=True
, each client (including
D
) invokes
\mathtt{PRF}

h(3d+1)
times, and performs
h(3d+1)
additions, and performs polynomial evaluations linear with the total number of bins
h
, where each evaluation involves
O(d)
additions and
O(d)
multiplications (in step
12
of
\mathtt{JUS}
).

Step
6
involves only
D
whose cost in this step is constant, as it involves invoking a public key encryption,
\mathtt{PRF}
, and commitment only once. Furthermore,
D
: (a) invokes
2h\cdot m
instances of
\mathtt{VOPR}
(in steps
6
and
7
of
\mathtt{JUS}
), (b) invokes
\mathtt{PRF}

h(3d+1)
times (in step
9
of
\mathtt{JUS}
), and (c) performs
h(d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+1)
multiplications and
3h\cdot m\cdot d
additions (in step
10
of
\mathtt{JUS}
). If
Flag=False
, then
D
performs
O(h\cdot m\cdot d)
multiplications and additions (in step
13
of
\mathtt{JUS}
).

Auditor’s Cost.

If
Flag=False
, then
{Aud}
invokes
\mathtt{PRF}

3h\cdot m(d+1)
times and invokes
\mathtt{H}

O(h\cdot m\cdot d)
times (in step
13
of
\mathtt{JUS}
).

Extractor’s Cost.

In step
9
, each extractor invokes the commitment scheme linear with the number of its set cardinality
|S|
and constructs a Merkle tree on top of the commitments. In step
11
, each extractor invokes
\mathtt{H}
linear with its set cardinality
|S|
; it also performs polynomial evaluations linear with
|S|
.

Smart Contracts’ Cost.

In step
10
, the subroutine smart contract
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
performs
h\cdot m(3d+1)
additions and
h
polynomial divisions, where each division includes dividing a polynomial of degree
3d+1
by a polynomial of degree
1
(in step
11
of
\mathtt{JUS}
). In step
12a
,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
invokes the commitment’s verification algorithm
\mathtt{Ver}
once,
\mathtt{H}
at most
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|
times, and
\mathtt{PRF}

|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|(3d+1)
times. In step
12b
,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
invokes
\mathtt{Ver}
at most
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|
times, and calls
\mathtt{H}

O(|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|S|)
times. In the same step, it performs polynomial evaluation linear with
|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|
. Thus, its overall complexity is
O(|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|(d+\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|S|))
.

9.2
Communication Cost

In steps
1
and
7
, the communication cost of the clients is dominated by the cost of
\mathtt{CT}
which is
O(m)
. In steps
2
–
6
, the clients’ cost is negligible, as it involves sending a few transactions to the smart contracts, e.g.,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
,
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
, and
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. Step
9
involves only extractors whose cost is
O(h)
as each of them only sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
a single value for each bin. In step
10
, the clients’ cost is dominated by
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s cost; specifically, each pair of client and
D
invokes
\mathtt{VOPR}

O(d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
times for each bin; therefore, the cost of each client (excluding
D
) is
O(h\cdot d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\cdot\bar{\xi})
while the cost of
D
is
O(h\cdot d^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\cdot\bar{\xi}\cdot m)
, where
\bar{\xi}
is the subroutine
\mathtt{OLE}
’s security parameter.
Step
11
involves only the extractors, where each extractor’s cost is dominated by the size of the Merkle tree’s proof it sends to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
, i.e.,
O(|S_{\scriptscriptstyle\cap}|\cdot\log_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|S|)
, where
|S|
is the extractor’s set cardinality.
In step
13
,
{Aud}
sends
h
polynomials of degree
3d+1
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
; thus, its complexity is
O(h\cdot d)
.
The rest of the steps impose negligible communication costs.

9.3
Comparison

Below we show that
\mathtt{ANE}
offers various features that the state-of-the-art PSIs do not offer simultaneously while keeping its overall overheads similar to the efficient PSIs.

Computation Complexity.

The computation complexity of
\mathtt{ANE}
is similar to that of PSI in
[
2
]
, but is better than the multiparty PSI’s complexity in
[
35
]
as the latter’s complexity is quadratic with the number of parties, i.e.,
O(|S|\cdot d\cdot m)
versus
O(|S|\cdot m^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+|S|\cdot m)
. Also,
\mathtt{ANE}
’s complexity is better than the complexity of the PSI in
[
39
]
that is quadratic with parameter
t
. Similar to the two-party PSIs in
[
15
,
42
]
,
\mathtt{ANE}
’s complexity is linear with
|S|
. The two-party PSI in
[
17
]
imposes a higher computation overhead than
\mathtt{ANE}
does, as its complexity is quadratic with sets’ cardinality. Hence, the complexity of
\mathtt{ANE}
is: (i) linear with the set cardinality, similar to the above schemes except the one in
[
17
]
and (ii) linear with the total number of parties, similar to the above multi-party schemes, except the one in
[
35
]
.
Hence, the computation complexity of
\mathtt{ANE}
is linear with the set cardinality and the number of parties, similar to the above schemes except for the ones in
[
35
,
17
]
whose complexities are quadratic with the set cardinality or the number of parties.

Communication Complexity.

\mathtt{ANE}
’s communication complexity is slightly higher than the complexity of the PSI in
[
2
]
, by a factor of
d\cdot\bar{\xi}
. However, it is better than the PSI’s complexity in
[
35
]
as the latter has a complexity quadratic with the number of parties.
\mathtt{ANE}
’s complexity is slightly higher than the one in
[
39
]
, by a factor of
d
. Similar to the two-party PSIs in
[
15
,
42
,
17
]
,
\mathtt{ANE}
’s complexity is linear with
c
.
Therefore, the communication complexity of
\mathtt{ANE}
is linear with the set cardinality and number of parties, similar to the above schemes except the one in
[
35
]
whose complexity is quadratic with the number of parties.

Features.

\mathtt{ANE}
is the only scheme that offers all the five features, i.e., supports fairness, rewards participants, is based on symmetric key primitives, supports multi-party, and is secure against active adversaries. After
\mathtt{ANE}
is the scheme in
[
39
]
which offers three of the above features. The rest of the schemes support only two of the above features. For the sake of fair comparison, we highlight that our
\mathtt{ANE}
and
\mathtt{JUS}
are the only PSIs that use smart contracts (that require additional but standard blockchain-related assumptions), whereas the rest of the above protocols do not use smart contracts.

10
Conclusion and Future Direction

Private Set Intersection (PSI) is a crucial protocol with numerous real-world applications. In this work, we proposed, Justitia, the first multi-party fair PSI that ensures that either all parties get the result or if the protocol aborts in an unfair manner, then honest parties will receive financial compensation. We then upgraded it to Anesidora, the first PSI ensuring that honest parties who contribute their private sets receive a reward proportionate to the number of elements they reveal. Since an MPC that rewards participants for contributing their private inputs would help increase its real-world adoption, an interesting open question is:

How can we generalise the idea of rewarding participants to MPC?

Appendix 0.A
Hash Tables

We set the table’s parameters appropriately to ensure the number of elements in each bin does not exceed a predefined capacity. Given the maximum number of elements
c
and the bin’s maximum size
d
, we can determine the number of bins by analysing hash tables under the balls into bins model
[
8
]
.

Theorem 0.A.1

(Upper Tail in Chernoff Bounds)
Let
X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
be a random variable defined as
X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle c}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}Y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, where
Pr[Y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=1]=p_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
,
Pr[Y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=0]=1-p_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, and all
Y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
are independent. Let the expectation be
\mu=\mathrm{E}[X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}]=\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle h}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}p_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, then

Pr[X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}>d=(1+\sigma)\cdot\mu]<\Big(\frac{e^{\scriptscriptstyle\sigma}}{(1+\sigma)^{\scriptscriptstyle(1+\sigma)}}\Big)^{\scriptscriptstyle\mu},\forall\sigma>0

In this model, the expectation is
\mu=\frac{c}{h}
, where
c
is the number of balls and
h
is the number of bins. The above inequality provides the probability that bin
i
gets more than
(1+\sigma)\cdot\mu
balls. Since there are
h
bins, the probability that at least one of them is overloaded is bounded by the union bound:

Pr[\exists i,X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}>d]\leq\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle h}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}Pr[X_{\scriptscriptstyle i}>d]=h\cdot\Big(\frac{e^{\scriptscriptstyle\sigma}}{(1+\sigma)^{\scriptscriptstyle(1+\sigma)}}\Big)^{\scriptscriptstyle\frac{c}{h}}

(4)

Thus, for a hash table of length
h=O(c)
, there is always an
almost constant
expected number of elements,
d
, mapped to the same bin with a high probability
[
40
]
, e.g.,
1-2^{\scriptscriptstyle-40}
.

Appendix 0.B
Enhanced OLE’s Ideal Functionality and Protocol

The PSIs proposed in
[
24
]
use an enhanced version of the
\mathtt{OLE}
. The enhanced
\mathtt{OLE}
ensures that the receiver cannot learn anything about the sender’s inputs, in the case where it sets its input to
0
, i.e.,
c=0
. The enhanced
\mathtt{OLE}
’s protocol (denoted by
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
) is presented in Figure
6
. In this paper,
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
is used as a subroutine in
\mathtt{VOPR}
, in Section
5.1
.

1.

Receiver (input
c\in\mathbb{F}
): Pick a random value,
r\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}
, and send
(\mathtt{inputS},(c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1},r))
to the first
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}}
.

2.

Sender (input
a,b\in\mathbb{F}
): Pick a random value,
u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}
, and send
(\mathtt{inputR},u)
to the first
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}}
, to learn
t=c^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\cdot u+r
. Send
(\mathtt{inputS},(t+a,b-u))
to the second
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}}
.

3.

Receiver: Send
(\mathtt{inputR},c)
to the second
\mathcal{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{OLE}}
and obtain
k=(t+a)\cdot c+(b-u)=a\cdot c+b+r\cdot c
. Output
s=k-r\cdot c=a\cdot c+b
.

Figure 6:

Enhanced Oblivious Linear function Evaluation (
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
)
[
24
]
.

Appendix 0.C
Counter Collusion Contracts

In this section, we present Prisoner’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
), Colluder’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
), Traitor’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
) originally proposed by Dong
et al.

[
18
]
. As we previously stated, we have slightly adjusted the contracts. We will highlight the adjustments in blue. For the sake of completeness, below we restate the parameters used in these contracts and their relation.

\bullet

\ddot{b}
: the bribe paid by the ringleader of the collusion to the other
server in the collusion agreement, in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
.

\bullet

\ddot{c}
: a server’s cost for computing the task.

\bullet

\ddot{ch}
: the fee paid to invoke an auditor for recomputing a task and resolving
disputes.

\bullet

\ddot{d}
: the deposit a server needs to pay to be eligible for getting the job.

\bullet

\ddot{t}
: the deposit the colluding parties need to pay in the collusion agreement, in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
.

\bullet

\ddot{w}
: the amount that a server receives for completing the task.

\bullet

\ddot{w}\geq\ddot{c}
: the server would not accept underpaid jobs.

\bullet

\ddot{ch}>2\ddot{w}
: If it does not hold, then there would be no need to use the servers and the auditor would do the computation.

\bullet

(pk,sk)
: an asymmetric-key encryption’s public-private key pair belonging to the auditor.

The following relations need to hold when setting the contracts
in order for the desirable equilibria to hold:
(i)
\ddot{d}>\ddot{c}+\ddot{ch}
, (ii)
\ddot{b}<\ddot{c}
, and (iii)
\ddot{t}<\ddot{w}-\ddot{c}+2\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}-\ddot{b}
.

0.C.1
Prisoner’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
)

\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
has been designed for outsourcing a certain computation. It is signed by a client who delegates the computation and two other parties (or servers) who perform the computation. This contract tries to incentivize correct computation by using the following idea. It requires each server to pay a deposit before the computation is delegated. If a server behaves honestly, then it can withdraw its deposit. However, if a server cheats (and is detected), its deposit is transferred to the client. When one of the servers is honest and the other one cheats, the honest server receives a reward. This reward is taken from the deposit of the cheating server. Hence, the goal of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
is to create a Prisoner’s dilemma between the two servers in the following sense. Although the servers may collude with each other (to cut costs and provide identical but incorrect computation results) which leads to a higher payoff than both behaving honestly, there is an even higher payoff if one of the servers manages to persuade the other server to collude and provide an incorrect result while itself provides a correct result. In this setting, each server knows that collusion is not stable as its counterparty will always try to deviate from the collusion to increase its payoff. If a server tries to convince its counterparty (without a credible and enforceable promise), then the latter party will consider it as a trap; consequently, collusion will not occur. Below, we restate the contract.

1.

The contract is signed by three parties; namely, client
{D}
and two other parties
E_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
E_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. A third-party auditor will resolve any dispute between
{D}
and the servers.
The address of another contract, called
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
, is hardcoded in this contract.

2.

The servers agree to run computation
f
on input
x
, both of which have been provided by
{D}
.

3.

The parties agree on three deadlines
(T_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},T_{\scriptscriptstyle 3})
, where
T_{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}>T_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
.

4.

{D}
agrees to pay
\ddot{w}
to each server for the correct and on-time computation. Therefore,
{D}
deposits
2\cdot\ddot{w}
amount in the contract.
This deposit is transferred from
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
to this contract
.

5.

Each server deposits
\ddot{d}^{\prime}=

\ddot{d}
\ +\ \ddot{X}
amount in the contract.

6.

The servers must pay the deposit before
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
. If a server fails to meet the deadline, then the contract would refund the parties’ deposit (if any) and terminates.

7.

The servers must deliver the computation’s result before
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

8.

The following steps are taken when (i) both servers provided the computation’s result or (ii) deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
elapsed.

(a)

if both servers failed to deliver the computation’s result, then the contract transfers their deposits to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

(b)

if both servers delivered the result, and the results are equal, then (after verifying the results)
this contract
must pay the agreed
amount
\ddot{w}
and refund the deposit
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
to each server.

(c)

otherwise,
{D}
raises a dispute with the auditor.

9.

When a dispute is raised, the auditor (which is independent of
{Aud}
in
\mathtt{JUS}
) re-generates the computation’s result,
by using algorithm
\mathtt{resComp}(.)
described shortly in Appendix
0.C.1
.
Let
y_{\scriptscriptstyle t},y_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},
and
y_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
be the result computed by the auditor,
E_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
, and
E_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
respectively. The auditor uses the following role to identify the cheating party.

\bullet

if
E_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
failed to deliver the result (i.e.,
y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is null), then it has cheated.

\bullet

if a result
y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
has been delivered before the deadline and
y_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\neq y_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
, then
E_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
has cheated.

The auditor sends its verdict to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
.

10.

Given the auditor’s decision, the dispute is settled according to the following rules.

\bullet

if none of the servers cheated, then
this contract
transfers to each server (i)
\ddot{w}
amount for performing the computation and (ii) its deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount. The client also pays the auditor
\ddot{ch}
amount.

\bullet

if both servers cheated, then
this contract (i) pays the auditor the total amount of
\ddot{ch}
, taken from the servers’ deposit, and (ii) transfers to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
the rest of the deposit, i.e.,
2\cdot\ddot{d}^{\prime}-\ddot{ch}
amount.

\bullet

if one of the servers cheated, then
this contract (i) pays the auditor the total amount of
\ddot{ch}
, taken from the misbehaving server’s deposit, (ii) transfers the honest server’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount) back to this server, (iii) transfers to the honest server
\ddot{w}+\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount (which covers its computation cost and the reward), and (iv) transfers to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
the rest of the misbehaving server’s deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{X}
amount.
The cheating server receives nothing.

11.

After deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
, if
{D}
has neither paid nor raised a dispute, then
this contract
pays
\ddot{w}
to each server which delivered a result before deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
and refunds each server its deposit, i.e.,
\ddot{d}^{\prime}
amount. Any deposit left after that will be transferred to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
.

Now, we explain why we have made the above changes to the original
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
of Dong
et al.

[
18
]
. In the original
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
(a) the client does not deposit any amount in this contract; instead, it directly sends its coins to a server (and auditor) according to the auditor’s decision, (b) the computation correctness is determined only within this contract (with the involvement of the auditor if required), and (c) the auditor simply re-generates the computation’s result given the computation’s plaintext inputs. Nevertheless, in
\mathtt{ANE}
, (1)
all clients
need to deposit a certain amount in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
and only the contracts must transfer the parties’ deposit, (2)
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
also needs to verify a part of the computation’s correctness without the involvement of the auditor and accordingly distribute the parties deposit based on the verification’s outcome, and (3) the auditor must be able to re-generate the computation’s result without being able to learn the computation’s plaintext input, i.e., elements of the set. Therefore, we have included the address of
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
to let the parties’ deposit move between the two contracts (if necessary) and allowed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
to distribute the parties’ deposit; thus, the requirements in points (1) and (2) are met. To meet the requirement in point (3) above, we have included a new algorithm, called
\mathtt{resComp}(.)
, in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. Shortly, we will provide more detail about this algorithm. Moreover, to make this contract compatible with
\mathtt{ANE}
, we increased the amount of each server’s deposit by
\ddot{X}
. Nevertheless, this adjustment does not change the logic behind
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
’s design and its analysis.

Auditor’s Result-Computation Algorithm.

In this work, we use
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
to delegate the computation of intersection cardinality to two extractor clients, a.k.a. servers in the original
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. In this setting, the contract’s auditor is invoked when an inconsistency is detected in step

∙

‣
13
of
\mathtt{ANE}
. For the auditor to recompute the intersection cardinality, we have designed
\mathtt{resComp}(.)
algorithm. The auditor uses this algorithm for every bin’s index
indx
, where
1\leq indx\leq h
and
h
is the hash table’s length. We present this algorithm in Figure
7
. The auditor collects the inputs of this algorithm as follows: (a) reads random polynomial
\bm{\zeta}
, and blinded polynomial
\bm{\phi}
from contract
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{JUS}}
, (b) reads the ciphertext if secret key
mk
from
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
, and (c) fetches public parameters
(des_{\mathtt{H}},h)
from the hash table’s public description.

Note that in the original
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
of Dong
et al.

[
18
]
, the auditor is assumed to be fully trusted. However, in this work, we have relaxed such an assumption. We have designed
\mathtt{ANE}
and
\mathtt{resComp}(.)
in such a way that even a semi-honest auditor cannot learn anything about the actual elements of the sets, as they have been encrypted under a key unknown to the auditor.

\mathtt{resComp}(\bm{\zeta},\bm{\phi},sk,ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk},indx,{des}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{H}})\rightarrow R

•

Input
.
\bm{\zeta}
: a random polynomial of degree
1
,
\bm{\phi}
: a blinded polynomial of the form
\bm{\zeta}\cdot(\bm{\epsilon}+\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
where
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
are arbitrary and pseudorandom polynomials respectively,
deg(\bm{\phi})-1=deg(\bm{\gamma}^{\prime})
,
sk
: the auditor’s secret key,
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
: ciphertext of
mk
which is a key of
\mathtt{PRF}
,
indx
: an input of
\mathtt{PRF}
, and
{des}_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{H}}
: a description of hash function
\mathtt{H}
.

•

Output
.
R
: a set containing valid roots of unblinded
\bm{\phi}
.

1.

decrypts the ciphertext
ct_{\scriptscriptstyle mk}
under key
sk
. Let
mk
be the result.

2.

unblinds polynomial
\bm{\phi}
, as follows:

(a)

re-generates pseudorandom polynomial
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
using key
mk
. Specifically, it uses
mk
to derive a key:
k=\mathtt{PRF}(mk,indx)
. Then, it uses the derived key to generate
3d+1
pseudorandom coefficients, i.e.,
\forall j,0\leq j\leq deg(\bm{\phi})-1:g_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=\mathtt{PRF}(k,j)
. Next, it uses these coefficients to construct polynomial
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle deg(\bm{\phi})-1}g_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
.

(b)

removes the blinding factor from
\bm{\phi}
. Specifically, it computes polynomial
\bm{\phi}^{\prime}
of the following form
\bm{\phi}^{\prime}=\bm{\phi}-\bm{\zeta}\cdot\bm{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

3.

extracts roots of polynomial
\bm{\phi}^{\prime}
.

4.

finds valid roots, by (i) parsing each root
\bar{e}
as
(e_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},e_{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
with the assistance of
des_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{H}}
and (ii) checking if
e_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=\mathtt{H}(e_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})
. It considers a root valid, if this equation holds.

5.

returns set
R
containing all valid roots.

Figure 7:
Auditor’s result computation,
\mathtt{resComp}(.)
, algorithm

0.C.2
Colluder’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
)

Recall that
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
aimed at creating a dilemma between the two servers. However, this dilemma can be addressed if they can make an enforceable promise. This enforceable promise can be another smart contract, called Colluder’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
). This contract imposes additional rules that ultimately would affect the parties’ payoffs and would make collusion the most profitable strategy for the colluding parties. In
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
, the party who initiates the collusion would pay its counterparty a certain amount (or bribe) if both follow the collusion and provide an incorrect result of the computation to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. Note,
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
requires both servers to send a fixed amount of deposit when signing the contract. The party who deviates from collusion will be punished by losing the deposit. Below, we restate
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
.

1.

The contract is signed between the server who initiates the collusion, called ringleader (LDR) and the other server called follower (FLR).

2.

The two agree on providing to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
a different result
res^{\prime}
than a correct computation of
f
on
x
would yield, i.e.,
res^{\prime}\neq f(x)
. Parameter
res^{\prime}
is recorded in this contract.

3.

LDR and FLR deposit
\ddot{t}+\ddot{b}
and
\ddot{t}
amounts in this contract respectively.

4.

The above deposit must be paid before the result delivery deadline in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, i.e., before deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
. If this condition is not met, the parties’ deposit in this contract is refunded and this contract is terminated.

5.

When
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
is finalised (i.e., all the results have been provided), the following steps are taken.

(a)

both follow the collusion
: if both LDR and FLR provided
res^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, then
\ddot{t}
and
\ddot{t}+\ddot{b}
amounts are delivered to LDR and FLR respectively. Therefore, FLR receives its deposit plus the bribe
\ddot{b}
.

(b)

only FLR deviates from the collusion
: if LDR and FLR provide
res^{\prime}
and
res^{\prime\prime}\neq res^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
respectively, then
2\cdot\ddot{t}+\ddot{b}
amount is transferred to LDR while nothing is sent to FLR.

(c)

only LDR deviates from the collusion
: if LDR and FLR provide
res^{\prime\prime}\neq res^{\prime}
and
res^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
respectively, then
2\cdot\ddot{t}+\ddot{b}
amount is sent to FLR while nothing is transferred to LDR.

(d)

both deviate from the collusion
: if LDR and FLR deviate and provide any result other than
res^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, then
2\cdot\ddot{t}+\ddot{b}
amount is sent to LDR and
\ddot{t}
amount is sent to FLR.

We highlight that the amount of bribe a rational LDR is willing to pay is less than its computation cost (i.e.,
\ddot{b}<\ddot{c}
); otherwise, such collusion would not bring a higher payoff. We refer readers to
[
18
]
for further discussion.

0.C.3
Traitor’s Contract (
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
)

\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
incentivises a colluding server (who has had signed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
) to betray its counterparty and report the collusion without being penalised by
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. The Traitor’s contract promises that the reporting server will not be
punished by
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
which makes it safe for the reporting server to follow the collusion strategy (of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
), and get away from the punishment imposed by
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. Below, we restate
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
.

1.

This contract is signed among
{D}
and the traitor server (TRA) who reports the collusion. This contract is signed only if the parties have already signed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
.

2.

{D}
signs this contract only with the first server who reports the collusion.

3.

The traitor TRA must also provide to this contract the result of the computation, i.e.,
f(x)
. The result provided in this contract could be different than the one provided in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, e.g., when TRA has to follow
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
and provide an incorrect result to
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
.

4.

D
needs to pay
\ddot{w}\ +\

\ddot{d}^{\prime}\ +

\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount to this contract. This amount equals the maximum amount TRA could lose in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
plus the reward.
This deposit will be transferred via
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
to this contract.
TRA must deposit in this contract
\ddot{ch}
amount to cover the cost of resolving a potential dispute.

5.

This contract should be signed before the deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
for the delivery of the computation result in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
. If it is not signed on time, then this contract would be terminated and any deposit paid will be refunded.

6.

It is required that the TRA provide the computation result to this contract before the above deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.

7.

If this contract is fully signed, then during the execution of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
,
{D}
always raises a dispute, i.e., takes step
8c
in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
.

8.

After
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
is finalised (with the involvement of the auditor), the following steps are taken to pay the parties involved.

(a)

if none of the servers cheated in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
(according to the auditor), then amount
\ddot{w}\ +\

\ddot{d}^{\prime}\ +

\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
is refunded to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
and TRA’s deposit (i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount) is transferred to
{D}
. Nothing is paid to TRA.

(b)

if in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, the other server did not cheat and TRA cheated; however, TRA provided a correct result in this contract, then

\ddot{d}^{\prime}\ +

\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount is transferred to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Also, TRA gets its deposit back (i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount) plus
\ddot{w}
amounts for providing a correct result to this contract.

(c)

if in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
, both servers cheated; however, TRA delivered a correct computation result to this contract, then TRA gets its deposit back (i.e.,
\ddot{ch}
amount), it also receives
\ddot{w}\ +\

\ddot{d}^{\prime}\ +

\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
amount.

(d)

otherwise,
\ddot{w}\ +\

\ddot{d}^{\prime}\ +

\ddot{d}-\ddot{ch}
and
\ddot{ch}
amounts are transferred to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
and TRA respectively.

9.

If TRA provided a result to this contract, and deadline
T_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
(in
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{PC}}}
) has passed, then all deposits, if any left, will be transferred to TRA.

TRA must take the following three steps to report collusion: (i) it waits until
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
is signed by the other server, (ii) it reports the collusion to
{D}
before signing
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
, and (iii) it signs
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{CC}}}
only after it signed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
with
{D}
. Note, the original analysis of
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
does not require
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
to remain secret. Therefore, in our smart PSI, parties TRA and
{D}
can sign this contract and then store its address in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. Alternatively, to keep this contract confidential,
{D}
can deploy a template
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
to the blockchain and store the commitment of the contract’s address (instead of the plaintext address) in
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
. When a traitor (TRA) wants to report collusion, it signs the deployed
\mathcal{SC}_{\scalebox{.52}{{TC}}}
with
{D}
which provides the commitment opening to TRA. In this case, at the time when the deposit is distributed, either
{D}
or TRA provides the opening of the commitment to
\mathcal{SC}_{\mathtt{ANE}}
which checks whether it matches the commitment. If the check passes, then it distributes the deposit as before.

Appendix 0.D
Proof of Theorem
5.2

Below, we restate the proof of Theorem
5.2
, taken from
[
3
]
.

Proof

Let
P=\{p_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,p_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\}
and
Q=\{q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},...,q_{\scriptscriptstyle t^{\prime}}\}
be the roots of polynomials
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
respectively. By the Polynomial Remainder Theorem, polynomials
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
can be written as
\mathbf{p}(x)=\mathbf{g}(x)\cdot\prod\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t}(x-p_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
and
\mathbf{q}(x)=\mathbf{g}^{\prime}(x)\cdot\prod\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle t^{\prime}}(x-q_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
respectively, where
\mathbf{g}(x)
has degree
d-t
and
\mathbf{g}^{\prime}(x)
has degree
d^{\prime}-t^{\prime}
. Let the product of the two polynomials be
\mathbf{r}(x)=\mathbf{p}(x)\cdot\mathbf{q}(x)
. For every
p_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in P
, it holds that
\mathbf{r}(p_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
. Because (a) there exists no non-constant polynomial in
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
that has a multiplicative inverse (so it could cancel out factor
(x-p_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
of
\mathbf{p}(x)
) and (b)
p_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is a root of
\mathbf{p}(x)
. The same argument can be used to show for every
q_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in Q
, it holds that
\mathbf{r}(q_{\scriptscriptstyle i})=0
. Thus,
\mathbf{r}(x)
preserves roots of both
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{r}
does not have any other roots (than
P
and
Q
). In particular, if
\mathbf{r}(\alpha)=0
, then
\mathbf{p}(\alpha)\cdot\mathbf{q}(\alpha)=0
. Since there is no non-trivial divisors of zero in
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}[X]
(as it is an integral domain), it must hold that either
\mathbf{p}(\alpha)=0
or
\mathbf{q}(\alpha)=0
. Hence,
\alpha\in P
or
\alpha\in Q
.
\Box

Appendix 0.E
Error Probability

Recall that in
\mathtt{JUS}
, in step
5
, each client
C
needs to ensure polynomials
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
do not contain any zero coefficient. This check ensures that client
C
(the receiver in
\mathtt{VOPR}
) does not insert any zero values to
\mathtt{VOPR}
(in particular to
\mathtt{OLE}^{\scriptscriptstyle+}
which is a subroutine of
\mathtt{VOPR}
). If a zero value is inserted in
\mathtt{VOPR}
, then an honest receiver will learn only a random value and more importantly cannot pass
\mathtt{VOPR}
’s verification phase.
Nevertheless, this check can be removed from step
5
, if we allow
\mathtt{JUS}
to output an error with a small probability.
7
7

7

By error we mean even if all parties are honest,
\mathtt{VOPR}
halts when an honest party inserts
0
to it.
In the remainder of this section, we show that this probability is negligible.

First, we focus on the product
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
. We know that
\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
is of the form
\prod\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle d}_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}(x-s^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
, where
s^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is either a set element
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
or a random value. Thus, all of its coefficients are non-zero. Also, the probability that at least one of the coefficients of
d
-degree random polynomial
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
equals
0
is at most
\frac{d+1}{p}
. Below, we state it formally.

Theorem 0.E.1

Let
\bm{\delta}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle t=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle d}u_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle t}
, where
u_{\scriptscriptstyle t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, for all
t,0\leq t\leq d
. Then, the probability that at least one of the coefficients equals
0
is at most
\frac{d+1}{p}
, i.e.,

Pr[\exists u_{\scriptscriptstyle t},u_{\scriptscriptstyle t}=0]\leq\frac{d+1}{p}

Proof

The proof is straightforward. Since
\bm{\delta}
’s coefficients are picked uniformly at random from
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
, the probability that
u_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
equals
0
is
\frac{1}{p}
. Since
\bm{\delta}
is of degree
d
, due to the union bound, the probability that at least one of
c_{\scriptscriptstyle t}
s equals
0
is at most
\frac{d+1}{p}
.

\Box

Next, we show that the probability that the polynomial
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
has at least one zero coefficient is negligible in the security parameter; we assume polynomial
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
has no zero coefficient.

Theorem 0.E.2

Let
\bm{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle m}a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle i}
and
\bm{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle n}b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\$}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
and
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i},b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\neq 0
, for all
i,j,0\leq i\leq m
and
0\leq j\leq n
. Also, let
\bm{\gamma}=\bm{\alpha}\cdot\bm{\beta}=\sum\limits_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}^{\scriptscriptstyle m+n}c_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\cdot x^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
. Then, the probability that at lest one of the coefficients of polynomial
\bm{\gamma}
equals
0
is at most
\frac{m+n+1}{p}
, i.e.,

Pr[\exists c_{\scriptscriptstyle j},c_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0]\leq\frac{m+n+1}{p}

Proof

Each coefficient
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
of
\bm{\gamma}
can be defined as
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle j=0\\
\scriptscriptstyle i=0\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle i=m\\
\scriptscriptstyle j=n\end{subarray}}a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
i+j=k
. We can rewrite
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
as
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=a_{\scriptscriptstyle w}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle j=0,j\neq z\\
\scriptscriptstyle i=0,i\neq w\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle i=m\\
\scriptscriptstyle j=n\end{subarray}}a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
, where
w+z=i+j=k
.
We consider two cases for each
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
:

\bullet

Case 1:
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle j=0,j\neq z\\
\scriptscriptstyle i=0,i\neq w\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle i=m\\
\scriptscriptstyle j=n\end{subarray}}a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0
. This is a trivial case, because with the probability of
1
it holds that
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=a_{\scriptscriptstyle w}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq 0
, as by definition
a_{\scriptscriptstyle w},b_{\scriptscriptstyle z}\neq 0
and
\mathbb{F}_{\scriptscriptstyle p}
is an integral domain.

\bullet

Case 2:
q=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle j=0,j\neq z\\
\scriptscriptstyle i=0,i\neq w\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle i=m\\
\scriptscriptstyle j=n\end{subarray}}a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\neq 0
. In this case, for event
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=a_{\scriptscriptstyle w}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle z}+q=0
to occure,
a_{\scriptscriptstyle w}\cdot b_{\scriptscriptstyle z}
must equal the additive inverse of
q
. Since
a_{\scriptscriptstyle w}
has been picked uniformly at random, the probability that such an even occurs is
\frac{1}{p}
.

The above analysis is for a single
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
. Thus, due to the union bound, the probability that at least one of the coefficients
c_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
equals
0
is at most
\sum\limits^{\scriptscriptstyle m+n}_{\scriptscriptstyle j=0}\frac{1}{p}=\frac{m+n+1}{p}
.

\Box

Next we turn out attention to
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
. Due to Theorem
0.E.1
, the probability that at least one of the coefficients of
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
equals
0
is at most
\frac{d+1}{p}
, .

Hence, due to Theorems
0.E.1
,
0.E.2
, and union bound, the probability that at least one of the coefficients in
\bm{\omega}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}\cdot\bm{\pi}^{\scriptscriptstyle{{(C)}}}
and
\bm{\rho}^{\scriptscriptstyle(C,D)}
equals
0
is at most
\frac{3d+2}{p}
, which is negligible is the security parameter
p
.
